CROWNING THE TEMPERANCE

QUEEN.

===== 0002601_19030501_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
Reetition of the Band g Hope

Festival.

On Monday evening Parr Hall was again the scene of festival promoted by the Warrington Band of Hope Union. So successful was the production of the pretty spectacle of "Crowning the Teraperanee Queen " a few weeks ago that the officials decided upon its repetition. The fact that there was again a large audience speaks volumes for the attractiveness of the entertainment, and the interest taken in the work of the Union. About 200 children, tastefully attired in white,occupied the platform gallery and •rendered the choruses. Mr. R. A. Naylor presided. And was accompanied by Miss Naylor. The proceedings oommenced with the singing of the hyrun " All people that on earth do dwell' and prayer was offered by Mr. W. H. Sherburne. The CHAIRMAN said it was a source of very great pleasure and gratification to him to be able to come from the great metropciiis for the purpose of attending that Band of Hope festival and to see so many young people and others present in that hall. The temper- ance question was, ifnet the greatest, one of the most important questions now before the consideration of the thinking public of this country,for if temperance did not prosper,the country would not prosper. If tem- perance increased the prosperity of the country would increase. Temperance and religion if practised by the I people of this country would cause the nation to ride upon the high places of the earth and to lead thenations of the world in the van of civilization and everything that was good and noble. The temperance movement! Ix ad now been paing on'for more than 60 years and they I had recently learnt a fact of which they ought really to be proud—that Warrington was the birthplace of that great and noble institution. (Applause.) He was fur- ther very proud t,olnow,that one of his relations—his mother's brother, Henry Andertou, the temperance poet, of Walton-le-Dale—ttsed to come to Warrington more than fifty years ago and deliver those wonderful orations to which thousands hadiistened in different parts of the country. He was proud-to know that he stood in the same position us Henry Anderton. (Applause.) He was a thorough going abstainer from all kinds of intoxicants and lived in a house where strong drink never entered. (Applause). He had been associated with the temperance movement and had tokenan interest in it for a great number of years, and he loved the work and the cause more than-ever. If they were to ask him,

what was the great force that kept him straight upon ! the question, be woiild-tell them that he could trace his family history back for 'GOO years. There was some-. thing in that history to be proud of,and something to be sorry for. He had had relations in the past—men of high intelligence sad capacity—and some of them had , gone down to the dogs and to beggary through strong drink. As be looked ,upon, the misery and wretchedness in connection withiais,own family history, what he said to them was a -solemn vow,—that he would neither touch, taste nor handle that which had proved such a .curse .to some of his pre- decessors. (Applanse.) It was a • pleasure to him to see the children present that night. They were dressed in white, and white meant temperance' virtue. Purity, and Christianity. Ile'hoped that the children would , always remain straight, true and good, and even influence their own parents, if therneeded influencing. Hestood before them that night with the firm conviction that the Angels were around them in that work, that the Great , Creator was with them, and that if Jesus Himself had been upon earth He would have come and helped them in that great work which was doing so much for the 'Advancement of His Kingdom here upon earth, for the glory of God and the good of humanity. He felt sure that God would bless the work and make those engaged ' ,-in it a blessing to those around them. (Applause). The cantata:" Crowning the Temperance Queen" then

-.commenced and proved -highly enjoyable. The fifty performers who engaged in it took their parts in a manner which reflected great credit upon all concerned, and called forth hearty applause. During the evening the Retiring Queen (Hattie Burgess, Bill Cliffs and Stockton Heath) and the Queen Elect (Barbara Oldroyd, St. James's) were pre- .sented with beautiful bouquets. There ware a couple of interludes during which an interesting miscellaneous . _

programme was subiuitted by juvenile artistes. Mastes "T. Barton, Cert. R.A.M., played the violin solos "Cavatine" (Adelmann) and 'Alice where art thou" in -excellent style and was heartily applauded. Miss 4-Carrie Haatings very sweetlyoang "The swallows" and -4` The voice of the Father'/ having the honour of a very 'hearty recall for the first-named. Masters Charles, ;Joseph and Arthur Nurse created much amusement by -their actionirecitation "The shoeblacks ",andja, dialogue -"Like ntothsr" by five girls from Buckley-street Band of Hope as well received. Miss Lavinia Hewitt, another promising young vocalist, delighted the audience with her expressive rendering of "A dolly and a coach" Andin response to an encore sang "Butterflies." -Scarf And tambourine drills were pleasingly executed

by girls from Padgate Band of Hope- The voices of Misses Lavinia. Hewitt • and Elsie Spruce blended beautifully in ' the duet "The violets" and an encore was accorded. Miss Elsie Spruce's feeling interpretation of "Daddy" met with the hearty approbation of the audience, and an encore was responded to. Master 3lcCollam provided the humorous items in the shape of the songs " When father laid the carpet on the stairs," and "The tramp." Both were so well given as to create great mirth, and verses of each had to be repeated. Before the close of the entertainment, books were presented by the newly-crowned queen to the following children in recognition of their services in selling a large number of tickets for the festival :—Arthur Whitfield, James Little, Eva Howard, Jessie Ellison, Minnie Whitmore, and Bertio Wakefield. Miss Naylor presented books to the following for ability displayed at the previous festival :—Barbara Oldroyd (queen elect), Hettie Burgess (retiring queen), Harry Griffiths (first herald), George A. Whitfield (door-keeper), Stanley Jones (second herald), and Albert Spruce (captain of guards of honour). Mr. A. Spruce again conducted the choir with ability and MT. F. Podinore, M.I.U.M. presided at the organ with praiseworthy taste and skill. On the proposition of Mr. SHERBURNE a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the Chairman and Miss Nayler,the conductor organist,all who had taken part in the entertainment and the mothers of the children for the interest they had taken in the event. Mr. NA.YLOR responded and moved a vote of thanks to the hon. secretary (Mr. F. Gandy) which was carried with acclamation.

BEWARE OF "SPRING MEDICINES."

CURE OF A-S. MAN WHOM DR. WILLIAMS'PINK PILLS SAVED FROM THE DIRE EFFECTS OF i AN 'OLD FASHIONED REMEDY.

PURGATIVES SHATTER HEALTH.

"Five years ago," said Mr. J. W. Smith, of 60 King's-' road, Hay Mills, Yardley, Birmingham, a white rash broke out -en .me in the spring. I attended the Skin Hospital for some time without result. An old lady whom I knew said that I needed Spring Medicine, and advised a mixture of Brimstone and Cream of Tartar; At the end of sixweeks the white marks had nearly dis- appeared. But the cure was dearly bought, for I was in a most advanced state of exhaustion." - And no wonder, if he had been purging himself for six weeks with sulphur and tartar, or any other open- ing medicine! I used," he continued, "to get up in the morning wheezing and absolutely gasping for breath, I was so weak. Three or .four hours would elapse before I could breathe with any comfort. I was never&ble to do any work until afternoon, when my lost vitality seemed— some of it—to return. I assure . you I was no more capable of working in the morning than of flying. My breath, too, was horrible—almost loathsome. This went on for some weeks despite the many medi- cines I tried. At last, I resolved to buy a box of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, which I had often read about in the papers. I soon began to feel the benefit. There was a surprising improvement from day to day, and after I had had three boxes I found it unnecessary to continue taking them any longer. I was completely caned." Mr. Smith had a fortunate escape. It is quite right to take a little Medicine in Spring to counteract the bad effects of change of season: but not a Purgative. On the contmry, the reason that spots break out on the skin at this time of the year, and that many people feel run down, is that the blood needs building up. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People do far more good than ordinary Spring Medicines, and they strengthen instead of weaken. Liver-spots, eczema, bile, indiges- tion, kieney complaints, languor, fatigue, anemia, and St. Vitus' dance are all 'liable to show themselves at this season of the year. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have cured them all. If any difficulty is experienced in ob- taining the genuine piils, which bear the full name in print on the wrapper, readers are adVised to send 25.9 d. to Dr. Williams' Medicine 'Company. Holborn Viaduct, London, who will send the pills - post-free; six boxes for 13s. 9d. Do not waib to be really ill. You need them now as a safe Spring Medicine, the only Spring Medicine that Strengthens instead of Weakening.

MARRIAGE AT LYNN. Percival—Massey.

A marriage of considerable 'local 'interest was ademnized at Lymra Parish Church on Tuesday, the contracting parties being Miss Martha Ellen Massey, daughter of the late Mr. Peter Massey, of Oierryilane, Lyzarn, and Mr. Joseph Percival of 'Crowley. The bride was becomingly attired in a handsome white dress with veil, and carried a pretty bouquet, and were a locket and chain, the gifts of the bridegroom. Her brides- maids were Miss Caroline Forster Massey (sister) and- Miss Percival, (of Norton), who were attiredin dresses of Pale blue, with black hats, and wore curb bracelets, the grits of the bridegroom. Mr. W.. Beswick, of Arley, discharged the duties of best man. The officiating clergyman was the rector (the Rev,. G. B. Thurston), and as the newly married couple left the church the bells rang out a merry peal. Later in ,the day the bride and bridegroom left for South- port, where the honeymoon will be spent. Numerous presents were received including the following :- -Mr. Dan Percival (Crowley), dinner set, cruet, and case .of carvers; Mr. and Mrs. Robert Brooks .(Appleton), knives; Miss Massey and Mr. Crosbie, clock; Mr. and Mrs. Bowyer (Helsby), decanters; Mr. and Mrs. W. Municipal Trading. On Monday afternoon a meeting of tile General Pur- poses Committee was held at the Town Hall under the presidency of the Mayor, when the names of the gentlemen who had been appointed to confer and draw up a scheme for an inquity into the trading trans- actions of the Corporation were approved, viz., Alder- man Bolton and Councillors Tinnion and James Evans to represent the Corporation, and Councillors Dodds, and Pemberton and Mr. S. Norbury-Williams, to represent the No-Municipal Trading Committee. Property owner 3 and the Corporation. At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Mr. S. Wilson, deputy town clerk, make an _application to the magistrates to fix May 14th for the hearing of objections by property owners to the forming and paving of certain streets and passages.. The as would probably occupy about two houra.--The.pagia- trates acceded to the request. •

MS BEDOWEN AT BAY,

In a wk Will Hannaford was out on bail. How it was managed. no one knew, as such a course is unusual when the crime is one so serious as murder. But the elder Hannaford was not without influence in political circles, and knew several members of the Government. Moreover, the evidence against Will was not so very strong. Had it not been for the enmity of Tait he woilid never have been committed for trial. " See here," said Mrs. Bedowen in a masterful manner, "I have had enough of this beating .about the bush. What do you know?" "I know. that you have blackmailed Tremewan for years."

- "Indeed. And on what ground?" "On the ground that you stated that Walter Pillage was married to Mrs. Tremewan before she inkrried your late master." " What of that? Tremewan married a widow." "A supposed widow," corrected Will; "you threatened to tell Mrs. Tremewan that her first husband was alive, and Tremewan, to close your mouth, not only took you on as housekeeper but left you this annuity in his will." Mrs. Bedowen rose and spread out her hands. "It is all perfectly true," she said, "and now that I have no further use for the secret you can have it. It seems that Pillage, the fool, told this to the Vane you speak of. Why?" "He thought he was dying at the time." "Ah!" taunted Mrs. Bedowen ironically, "he wished to whitewash his black soul, I suppose. Well, I don't care. The secret has served my turn. I did make my own terms with Tremewan on those grounds. Now I have my reward. I don't care if Mrs. Tremewari is turned out of the estates. She always treated me in *a haughty, peacock way, although I am a better woman than she ever was. As to your dear Dora-" "Don't say a word a.gainst her," cried Will, rising. "Have done with this talk! You admit that you used Tremewan's secret to blackmail him." "Some people might call it blackmail," replied Mrs. Bedowen indifferently. "Who told you this secret?" "Alii it seems you don:t know everything!" she sneered. "Well, then, I won't tell you. And you can't make me. I have my annuity. Try and upset the will if you can."

• "Ls Waiter Pillage alive Bow?" "I shan't tell you. I shall say nothing." "rit have you arrested," threatened Will,. • thoroughly angry. Mrs. Beclowen laughed in her most insulting' manner. "Try," she said, "Try I" Will rose to go. It was no use threatening this woman, as he could not fulfil his threats in any way. She was mistress of the situation, and was resolved to remain so. Mrs. Bedowen saw that he was helpless, and waved her hand ironically round the room. "All mine," she said, "all mine. I think I have done very well out of Mr. Tremewan. Don't you?"

"You are a. de/rill" said Will, losing his temper. The woman shrugged her shoulders contemp- tuously. "Hard names," said she in a bland tone. "I don't care. I've had plenty of them in my life. They will not take away my money, or my cottage, or my seat in the church. I have had a sad life, Mr. Will, but now I am respect, able. The clergyman himself is coming to call on me. Think of that!"

"Don't be too sure that your ill-earned wealth will remain," warned Will, as he walked towards the door. "I can have you arrested on sus-- picion of being concerned in this murder. You did not do it yourself. But you know who did." "I suppose you are thinking of Walter Pil- lage," replied the woman coolly ;" t'ut as yet I have not told you whether he is dead or alive." "Do you know?" asked -Will, pausing at the door.

Mrs. Bedowen tossed her head and laughed in quite an amiable manner. "I am sorry I oan't answer that question. But, then, it is not your business."

"I think it is very much my business,"_replied Will, grimly; ".you forget that I have a capital charge hanging over my head." "What has that to do with Walter Pillage being alive?" "Simply this. That I believe, he is alive, and that it was he who strangled Mr. Tremewan." He said no more, but walked away, feeling 'sure- that this was not a propitious moment for get- ting the truth out of Mrs. Bedowen. She stared after him frowning. " H'm," she said, under her breath, "you might make a. worse shot." •

A Dyspeptic

Like a Man in Gaol Until Relief Comes by

Dr. Morse's Indian Root Pills.

A MAN in gaol cannot follow his natural inclinations. His enjoyment is limited... He cannot eat what he wants. He is limited to a very frugal diet. He is alive, to be sure, but life possesses few advantages. Are not all these things equally true of a dyspeptic? For all the real enjoy- ment he gets out of life he might as well be in gaol. He cannot eat what he likes, nor as much as he likes. If he transgresses any of the rules of his diet he is punished for it. Such was the case of Mr. B. Harvee, Cavendish Road, Harringay. He says: "So bad was I, to such a state had I come, that my friends, noticing my condition, warned me against consumption. EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

01-4 .617

TEE KUM'S VISIT TO ROJSE.

His Majesty's visit to Rome has excited perhaps as much interest as his call at Paris, and it is confidently hoped that the latter event will be quite as free from any discordant note as has been the case at all the other Places he has touched. His Majesty left Naples on Monday at half-past nine in the morning, and arrived at Rome at three in the afternoon. The Royal train was preceded by twenty minutes by an ex-press locomotive, and travelled at the rate oi 6o kilometres per hour. It was composed of four saloon cars belonging to the Italian Royal train and two carriages of the Mediterranean Railway Company, and was ,drawn by two locomotives. Queen Helena surrounded by her Court, received King Edward in the Grand Hall of the Swiss Guards in the Palace of the Quirinal. King Edward afte - wards visited Queen Margherita at her palace in the Via Veneto. There was family dinner in the evening in the Quirinal. On Tuesday the King and Queen of Italy entertained the King at luncheon, and in the evening King Edward received the heads of foreign missions. There was also on this day a State dinner, at which the Ministers were present, and a State performance was given in the Argentine Theatre. On Wednesday morning the King attended a military review. King Victor Emmanuel and the princes proceeded in car- riages, escorted by cuirassiers, as far as the barracks of the carabineers. Here they mounted on horseback and rode to the review ground to await the arrival of the Queen and King Edward, who left the Quirinal at 'half- past eight in an open carriage. At 12.30 a luncheon Was given to King Edward at the British Embassy, from which place his Majesty proceeded to the Vatican. At 8.30 p,m. there was a dinner at the British Embassy, at which the King and Queen of Italy were pre- sent. At noon on Thursday a luneheon was given at the Quirinal, after which his Majesty left for Paris. The Voce della Verita, in an- nouncing King Edward's visit to the Pope, recalled the fact that before King Edward's time Caedwallo, King of the Anglo-Saxons in the seventh century, went to Rome in order 'to be baptised.

THE FRENCH PRESIDENT'S VISIT. .

It- is very gratifying to those who are desi .tous of promoting good feeling between the English and French races to ha.ve President -Loubet visit London, so near to the time of Xing Edward's call at Paris. There can be =tittle doubt in the mind of the British public as a whole that France is a far More congenial friend than Ger- Many. The gut- ter press of Paris, Which seeks to stimulate ani- %ity between ^IL -Le races, need not be taken in to account : and it may be very safely said -that this ani- mosity is almost as purely imagi- native as re- gards the other side of the Chan- nel as we know it to be on this. England may laugh and even be disgusted at PRESIDENT LOUBET, the French, but there is little Worse ill-feeling than this. France is really doing nothing to merit our animosity com- pared to what Germany is doing and will do. Germany is anything but friendly to us; she Only seeks our aid to make us cat's-paw, as in the Venezuelan tiouble, to say nothing of the Perpetual raid she makes on the British labour market and British trade. France is flippant, but Germany is dangerous ; it is obvious Which is the more desirable. Hence, it should be 46ur aim to give President I.oubet a splendid reception, and it is gratifying to notice evi- dence of this being given

THE FATE OF LONDON'S LYCEUM.

For many months now the fate /f the Lyceum Theatre -has hung in the bala ce, and last week the climax came when the/old pile Was offered for sale by auction, but did not -evoke a large enough bid to be knocked down to anyone. The announcement of the sale 'had drawn together. a considerable company •of sympathetic non-buyers, who cheered the auctioneer's kindly references to Sir Henry Irving, and his expression of a hope that the - result of the sale might mean the preservation of the historic theatre which they all knew * so well. But capital was not demonstrative. , The property included the Theatre, the Beef- steak Room, and sundry shops, and it has frontages to Wellington Street, Burleigh Street, and Exeter Street, and an entrance from the Strand, the whole area being about 23, coo feet, or over half an acre. The auctioneer suggested that such a site must be worth from £l5 to 20 a 'foot, and said that taking the

former figure they had a value of £352,000, but he would ask for an offer of DOO,OOO to start with. There was •no response, however, and invitations to bid £250,000 and lower sums were not more successful. Li 50,000 was bid. Then by bids of £5,000 the amount offered reached o, 000. From this figure, the auctioneer, gently chiding "each dull delay," advances of £2,000 raised the bidding to 240,000, which the auctioner said was only a very little over iio a foot, and he had dealt with land in that quarter recently at £3O a foot. After th:# there were two more bids, but the competition . stopped at £244,000, and the auctioner, •observing that he was flat empowered to sell at that Price, bought the property in at L'260,000, to the disappointment of the "chorus," who al had from time to time applauded the tenew of offers; and had warmly greats() rho final bid as giving promise of mores

ROYALTY AND ART NEEDLEWORK.

The completion of the new building at South Kensington, in which the Royal School of Art Needlework have now taken up their perinament abode, adds one more to the num- ber of important edifices built by architects of to-day in the same neighbourhood. In point of size and ground area it cannot, of course, compare with its .three great neigh- bours—the Imperial Institute, the Natural History Museum, and the Victoria and Albert Museum. But it can rank in the front as an architectural feature. This may be due in part to the fact that it is a corner building; but still more because in animation, in colour, lesign, and style, it affords such a contrast to the adjacent buildings. Mr. F. B. Wade, the architect, has had to contend with many grave .lifficulties in its planning, not the least being that of the accommodation of a portion of the Zity and Guilds of the London !Technical

A ROYAL BETROTHAL.

Royal betrothals are always accorded a fair share of public interest, if only from the fact of their .comparatively rare occurence. The couplOnow occupying the most attention are the Grand Duke of Saxe-Weimar and Princess Caroline of Reuss, who will shortly go through the time-honoured ceremony at the altar of Hymen, The Grand Duke is not perhaps very well known to English 'news-

paper readers, but in his own circles he a most popular man, kind and generous to a fault, though perhaps a little impulsive, and with a remarkable dmount of insight into human nature. Verily, this is a love match, for everyone in the know avers that it was a case of "first sight" that was, moreoven not too strong to last. The affection of this royal couple is perfectly mutual, one side acing as pronounced as the other.- The mu- Sims takes place very shortly.

Baptist Church, Colborne street, On Sundays evening last , the Pastor preached to a ; large congregation, taking as his subject "Did Christ rise from the Dead ?" He gave as evidence in support - of, this theory. (1) The witness of the Gospels, which, • while differing in some matters of detail, all agree that Christ rose from the dead on the third day. (2) The witness of St. Paul. . (3) The silent witness of the first day of the week, which was recognised as the Christian Sabbath soon after the resurrection of •Christ and in commemoration of that event. The resurrection power, the preacher stated. in conclusion, still sustains the Church of Christ to-ay.

NORTHERN RUGBY UNION.

THE NORTHERN UNION CUP.

Final Tie.

Halifax Beat Salford.

The final .tie for the Northern Union Cup .was played at Headingley, Leeds, on Saturday before 32,000 people. A very hard game resulted. At half-time, there was no score, but soon after resuming Bartle scored for Halifax from a forward rush and Little kicked a goal. Hadwen next landed a pecalty goal, and Salford were defeated by 7 points to nil.

===== 0002601_19030501_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LEAGUE.---FIRST DIVISION.

WARRINGTON v. HUDDERSFIELD

There %is hardly as good a gate as usual on the Wilderspool enclosure to witness the last ”latch of the season. Huddersfield were the visitors and there would be a crowd of about 4,000 when hostilities were corn- menced. Teams : _ _ _ _

Warrington—Hallam, back; Fish, Isherwood, Dicken- son, and Harris, threequarter backs; Edmunds and Duckworth, half backs ; Boardman, Edmondson, Forster, Coleman, Naylor, W. Swift, Jolley, and J. Swift, for- wards.

Huddersfield—Kitchen, back ; Wimpenny, Thomas, Larrard, and Deere, threequarter backs • Holyroyd and Dewhurst, half backs ; Chambers, France, Knight, Littlewoon, Naylor, Hurst, Walker, and Sutcliffe, for- wards. Referee, Mr. Crompton,. Bradford,, Touch judges, Messrs. Critchley (Widnes) and Wightinan (Swinton.) - Sutcliffe kicked off and early on Holroyd initiated a good passing movement for Huddersfleld,but the effort was spoiled by a knock-on.. The Huddersfield forwards had the advantage in the Packs, and heeled out repeat- edly, but the backs made poor use of the openings which presented themselves. The match had not been in pro- gress many minutes, when Harris picked up the ball in the Huddersfield half, and after a magnificent run, which both Wimpenny and Kitchen tried to check, he forced himself over the line and scored a try near the corner flag. Dickenson failed at the goal kick. Larrard got away but was pulled up by Dickenson. Hudders- field were penalised,but fish failed to kick a goal from a somewhat easy place. At half-time Warrington were leading by a try to nil. Early in the second half, Fish intercepted a passing movement between Larrard and Deere, and racing away kicked the ball over the head of Kitchen ; and regain- ing possession scored a meritorious try. Dickenson's goal-kicking again failed: Huddersfield got down to the Warrington end, and a passing movement between Holroyd, Dewhurst, Thomas, and Wimpenny resulted in the last-named player scoring a try at the corner. Sut- cliffe failed at goal. Result: Warrington, two tries (6 points) ; Huddersfield, one try (3 points.)

===== 0002601_19030501_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
SWINTON 'lr. HULL.

Beautiful weather favotred the meeting of the above teams at Swinton. The visitors could not get the ball away, and Lewis snapping a pass from Valentine scored, Evans failing at goal. Half-time :—Swinton, one try; Hull,nothing.—On resuming, it was noticeable that Hampson was lame, and of little use, but Swinton immediately commenced to attack, and in a very short time both Valentine and Wallwork dashed over for the home side, and from one of the tries landed a goat. After this quick scoring,Rhodes dashed away, but the game was quickly transferred to the visitors' half again, and a fine piece of work by WallwOrk ended in the latter scoring a try, Evans landing the goal. Afterwards Hampson and Lewis scored, and in both instances Evans failed. Result :—Swinton, one goal five tries (19 points) ; Hull, nothing. BRIGHOUSE RANGERS v. BRADFORD.

At Brighouse. The home team was at full strength, but there were two alterations on the Bradford side. Result :—Bradford two coals, three tries (13 points); Brighouse two goals (4 points.)

===== 0002601_19030501_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
LEIGH v. OLDHAM.

At Leigh. While the game was even in the first half, Johnson kicking a goal for the home team and Ferguson accomplishing & similar performance for Oldham, Leigh were much too smart afterwards, and tries were scored by Ganley, Johnson, Bennett, and Roberts. Johnson kicked a goal, and the result was—Leigh, two goals, four tries (16 points); Oldham, one goal (2 points).

===== 0002601_19030501_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN v. WIGAN.

Played at Runcorn before a poor gate, the last match of the season witnessing a decline of interest in the doings of the club. Both played representative teams. Runcorn started against sun and wind, Woods—a fresh hand—being twice responsible for a determined attack on the visitors' line. Later Woods, Price, and Moran nearly got in a pass forward spoiling a splendid chance. A fine kick by Mason found touch in the home 25. Butterworth kicked the ball away but Mason marked in fine style in front of goal, the kick, however, failing. Uninteresting play followed, livened up by a neat sprint by Price, Jones failing to gather on the line. From the scrummage Jolly obtained and handed to Butterworth and Woods. Price completed the movement by scoring at the corner. Farmer failed at goal. Wigan attacked strongly and after scrambling play Isherwood kicked down the field. Price, Wood,and Moran failed to stop the onward rush of the visitin,g forwards. Isherwood at length secured and popped over at the corner, the goal kick failing. Wigan continued to press,and on two occasions had hard lines in falling to pierce the defence. , Jolly cleared. The visitors, however, again got to.work, Isherwood touching- down at the corner but,the point was disallowed on account of off-side play. .•Runcorn were still kept on the defensive, Harrison being applauded for good defensivework. Off- side play gave the visitors a chance, but Duffy failed to steer the ball over the bar. . Half-time, Runcorn, one 'try, Wigan, one try.—The resumption saw. the visitors in the home 25 where Jolly cleared. The attack was resumed, but Harrison. marked and sent the visitors back. Farmer next marked and shot at goal the ball striking the bar. A run by Price set the ball in motion and after scrambling play Wood good over. The ball, however, was brought back. Scrummaging in front of the Wigan line gave Jolly a rare chance which he took,and dropped a lovely goal.—Exchanges left the advantage with the hometown and for some time Runcorn held the. upper hand. Eventually Price and Jones ran the ball up the field where Wood secured and rushed over the line after a tricky sprint. Farmer missed an easy shot at goal. Woods next ran the ball up in gallant fashion forcing the game in the visitors'2s. Final,Runcorn, eight points ; Wigan, thret. points. •

===== 0002601_19030501_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
MONDAY'S MATCHES.-HALIFAX THE CHAMPIONS.

LEIGH v. HALIFAX.

Played on Monday at Leigh. Molyneux started for Leigh, who sent the ball into • touch near the Halifax quarter. Morley had his kick charged down, but Jones relieved by a kick into touch. A rush by the Halifax forwards was stopped by Pearson, and a kick by Moly- neux removed play to the Halifax quarter. Molyneux tried to drop a goal, but his kick was charged down. After some grand passing between J. Riley and Williams, Wedgwood knocked off Pearson and scored, the same player failing at goal. Leigh attacked after this, but found Little impassible and eventually, a rush of the Halifax forwards removed play to the Leigh half,where Kirk had a kick charged down and Rigg rushed up and transferred to Wedgwood who scored easily, but the same player failed at goal. At the interval Halifax , were -leading by two tries to nothing. In the second 1 half Leigh tried various methods to break through the Halifax defence, but found Little an impenetrable barrier. About two minutes from the close, from a scrummage near the Halifax line, Morley darted away 1 and kicked over the Leigh full back's head. An exciting race ensued, but the ball bounced right for the Halifax players, and after a lot of passing Wedgwood eventually got hold and scored behind the posts and also placed a goal. Leigh made a last effort to score, but were beaten back, and the game ended Halifax one goal and three tries (eleven points) ; Leigh nothing. HULL KINGSTON ROVERS v SALFORD.

Salford finished their list of fixtures with a match on Monday against Hull Kingston Rovers, in which they were badly beaten by no less than sixteen points'. This result gave Halifax' the championship. From the kick-off it was quickly noticed That the Rovers were abetter lot, and the game had scarcely been in progress five minutes, when, from a round ttf passing, West crossedthe Salford line, and scored a try, which Starks improved. Lomas was next conspicuous with a good run, but the Rovers quickly regained the lost ground, and another try was scored by Gordon. At half-time the Rovers led by eight points to nothing. Salford were Completely overplayed in the seccind half, and had another eight points scored against them, the final result being Kingston Rovers, two goats and four tries (16 points) ; Salford, nothing. •

A gruesome spectacle confronted two Accrington con- stables on Wednesday, when they forced a* entrance .into the house in Black Abbey-street. Lying unclothed on the floor was the body of a mechanic named Williath Holt, who had for two years been living alone. The man had evidently been dead for about a week. Oa the tables wale a number of bottles containing whisky. Holt had not been seen by the neighbours for eight or nine days. • •

WYCLIFFE SUNDAY SCHOOL ANNIVERSARY.

This annual event was celebrated on Sunday and was as successful as its predecessors. The attendance at the morning service was not as large as usual on the anniver- sary day, but there were exceptionally large congrega- tions at the afternoon and evening services. The com- munion table was adorned with a choice collection of irises and daffodils. The preacher for the occasion was the Rev. D. Lincoln Jones 8.A., "8.D., A.T.S., of Roby Chapel, Manchester. The Children who were accommo- dated in the galleries sang very sweetly a number of hymns, the infants under the conductor- ship of Mr. Joseph Owen, singing one hymn by themselves, entitled "The Golden Chain.' The choir gave an excellent rendering of the anthem "A day in Thy Courts" (Macfarren). The preacher based his discourse on the text "With what measure ye meet it shall be measured unto you" (Matthew chap. 7, ver. 2). That was Christ's way, he said, of enunciating a great truth. There was a law of recompense running through life, a law which none of us could possibly escape from and it worked out its end with inevitable regularity. from, was one of the things we could reckon on in life. He proceeded to show that human conduct I met with its own return, and that the world we live in was the projection of our own hearts. We made our own world, and the world gave back to us just what we gave to it. The importance of cultivating a right spirit was dwelt upon and the preacher showed how the law of recompense operated,regarding our relationship with God. He pointed out that God did not give his best gifts promiscuously; there were conditions attached to His best gifts. He gave them on condition that we gave something to Him. Our whole life would be transfigured if weimade this exchange.—ln the afternoon there was a combined meeting of Sunday scholars and members of the P.S.A. Society, and the church I was completely filled. The scholars attending Wycliffe I Cockhedge Mission were also present. The choir gallery was filled with the members of the Sunday school choir who greatly added to the effectiveness of the singing. Mr. William Brown, one of the secretaries, conducted and Mr. T. F. Wallington, the school organist, presided at the organ. Mr. George Wallington, superintendent of the school, presided and briefly addresitod the large congregation. Mr. William Beswick offered prayer and Mr. George Brown read the scripture lessons. The children acquitted themselves creditably in their ser- vice of song, the singing of the choral march "Warriors of the Lord" being specially well given. Miss Alice Griffiths very sweetly sang the solo "Come unto Me." Mrs. Hampson, of Warrington, delivered an address in which she referred to the importance of home influence in the training of children. Parents who early taught their children Christian principles had a sure reward.—At the evening service, the preacher based his discourse on the words 'For what is a man profited if he gain the whole world and lose or forfeit his own self.' (Luke 9, 25 ver). That question, he said, stated the issue that concerned every- I one of us in a manner which was calculated to startle and arouse the most careless. He proceeded to show that every human being was a compound of two distinct elements, and pointed out that they must draw the dis- tinction between what a man has with all his attain- ments and accomplishments as .opposed to what a man is. In addressing himself to the question of what was one's own self, he said that Christ had answered it by stating that one's own self was one's own soul. The soul was that which came from God and which linked us to God. The soul was conscience, and love and the sense of the eternity. It was that in us which lifted us above the animal and differentiated us from the brute. In a very able and thoughtful way the preacher showed that it was possible forthe soul to be lost and forfeited,and it often happened that men lost their own souls through gaining the world He illustrated his _point by referring to the Hiles of Esau, Lot and the Israelites. No temporal power, he contended, however, great could compensate for the loss of one's self. The world was a miserable bauble compared with the soul.—The choir sang the anthem "Sing 0 Heavens" (Sullivan) in an effective manner, the solo part being taken by Mr. A. Griifiths. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus Bac presided at the organ at the morn- ing and evening services. Collections were taken,on behalf of the school funds and realised £42 is. 9d. slightly less than last year.

TIRED OF LIFE. A Warrington Woman's Suicide.

Having told her sister that she was tired of life, Alice Gore, of no fixed abode, committed suicide by jumping into the Mersey at Warrington on Monday afternoon. The circumstances surrounding her death were detailed at the inquest which was conducted at the Police Station by Mr. S. Brighouse,County Coroner,on Tuesday evening. Mary Emmett, of no fixed abode, said the deceased was her sister, and was 38 years of age. She was the widow of John Gore, a collier. On Sunday she and her sister walked from Chester to Warrington, and stayed the night at Mrs. Davies's common lodging-house in Friar's-green. On Monday morning they wen,t out and had several drinks. At three o'clock in the lifternoon they went up Wilderspool-road and met a man named David Salt, who bought a pint of beer from a public- house close by.. They then proceeded along Chester-road where they drunk the beer. Witness and Salt then walked on in; front, leaving deceased behind. As deceased did not follow witness went back to her. Deceased said "I am tired of my lifeand I am going to drown myself." She then got over the railings, threw off her jacket and hat, and jumped into the river. Salt jumped in after her, but was seized with cramp and had to come out. Deceased had threatened to take away her life on Saturday. _

David Salt, living in Ship-yard, corroborated, and said he was only about a yard from the deceased when he was seized with cramp. William Warburton, cab driver, said he was driving his horse and cab along Chester-road, and saw deceased jump into the river. Salt had only waded in the water up to his hips when he'said he had got cramp. Deceased and her companions were under the influence of drink. James Knight, engine cleaner, New-road, spoke to recovering the body. Four constables rased artificial respiration, but without success.

tmmett ;'as recalled and stated that since the death of her husband three of deceased's children had died. A verdict of" Suicide whilst of unsound mind " was returned.

At Bow-street Police Court,iLondon, on Wedn.esd ay, Henry Fennimore George, a private detective of Fl ix- ton, Manchester,was formally committed for trial upon charges of perjury alleged to have been committed by him in the divorce suit of Worsley v. Worsley, known as the" Uncle Joe " case. 'Bail was allowed in two sureties of £3OO each.

An alarming fire broke out on Tuesday night on the premises of the International Salt Company; Chicago. The outbreak severely taxed the resources of the brigade, and despite all their efforts they could not save the vast storehouses. The entire plant was destroyed, together with 400,000 tons of salt and • three boats that were lying alongside the wharf. The loss is estimated at 1,250.000 dols.—" Central News." The calender for the forthcoming Liverpool Spring Assizes, which opens at St. George's Hall on Tuesday next, is of a rather grave character. Thirty prisoners, are, up to date, concerned in 23 cases, and the calendar contains four charges of murder, two from Liv rpool, One from Bury, and one from Southport.

THE large majority THE GOVERNMENT'S in favour of gr. BID FOR THE BUTCHER'S Pub 1 i" PUBLICANS' VOTE. cans' Compensation

Bill may justly b 0 regarded as another electioneering incident. IV action of the Conservstive majority follows pre- cisely on the lines laid down by the LORD CHAW- CELLOR by Mr. BALFOUR and by Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN in recent speeches and letters. The party and its leaders are firmly agreed that what has been called "the rot cannot be stopped until confidence is restored t) the minds of all manuer of persons who are interested in the trade in intoxicating liquors. The second reading of Mr. BUTCHER'S Bill, which was supported by the PRIME MINISTER, the COLONIAL SECRETARY and Mr. LONG, will, there can be little doubt, achieve that object. The measure has gone triumph- antly through what will prove to be its final stage. lt will not be heard of again this - session ; but a majority of two to one for the second reading, under the pressure of strong Government support, will be accepted by the public-house interest as a sufficient pledge that the party does not intend to be unfaithful to the trade interests of the most powerful section of its supporters. The Unionist party will never desert Mrs. MicalvnErt. The Bill of the member for York is a measure to prevent justices from do- ing what they have always been entitled to-d--o. They are at present engaged to some extent, and with variations in each borough and county, in carrying out the recommendations of a Ro-yal Commission. Tha majority of that Commission— not only the minority—declared that it regards the suppr3ssion of a large number of licenses as not only necessary, but essential. Essential for what? Essential for the public good. The whole system of licensing is based on the legal presumption that the trade in intoxicating liquors is a dangerous trade. The majority of the Royal Commission presumes that the danger becomes greater where there is an ex- cessive number of licensed houses in proportion to population. But this is not a presumption only. It would be borne out by any map which showed in what districts most *rime existed, and especially in what districts there are most crimes of violence. The refusal to renew licences where they are desionstrably !injurious to the public welfare is one of the most clearly-established rights of:the magistracy, whose primary function is the preservation of the peace. That a publican has not been convicted, or that objection cannot be brought against his methods of management, is an argument to which every bench of magistrates will give reasonable attention. But there can be no excuse for the continuance of licenses when they have ceased to be necessary to the conveni- ence or the comfort of the public. This is the position of the licensing justices as we tinders tand it, and as they only grant licenses for the year they are fully justified in withdrawing them when the year for which they were granted has come to an end. It must be admitted that amon g Englishmen there is a feeling that when a man has built up a business he should be compensated when for public reasons he is compelled to abandon it. The needs of justice in this matter are pro- bably met in Lord PEEL.S report. To raise a com- pensation fund from "the trade itself—certain licensed houses profiting by the suppression o others—must seem reasonable,even from the point of view of those who hold that no license should be extinguished without compensation. To give notice over a certain number of years that certain houses will be withdrawn at the end of that term is surely as much as can be expect ed from the justices representing the public interest. Any other manner of proceeding would create * permanent rested interest and enormously encou

===== 0002601_19030501_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
2golt that gambling in public-house property which has a late years become a public scandal. Neither the views expressed by members of the Government nor the proposals made by Mr. BUTCHER'S measure Proceed en these moderate but just lines. Mr. Butcher's Bill proposes to make the publican con; tribute to the suppression of licenses, but it con- tains provisions which would evidently result in laling the whole burden of responsibility on the tallPaYer. Among those who voted for the measure there must have been many who were fully cognis- nut of its evil features, but they knew that it Would not pass. They were intent on electioneer- ing demonstration. They wished to make it ap- Pear to the publican than CODLIN is his friend, 4/4 SHORT. As Mr. ASQUITH insisted, they mixed tile issues. The former HOME SECRETARY declared that the Hoase was. not in fact called upon to 'tote on the question of compensation, but on the Itilleiple of Mr. BUTCHER'S Bill, which is bad, and 'that exactly expresses the truth. The Govern- teent of false- pretences has won another victory 'O n another false pretence.

"Itis of no use having an Empire without an iruPenal race." The observation of Lord Rosebery the new number (Atha Nineteenth Century serves % text for a warning ai to the deterioration of t!r oattonal physique. The writer brings out very 114111 the difference in the health and stamina of Ap,"tarih and German soldiers as shown by the reports for the year before the South Afri- -80111,,we.r. The admissions to hospital of British .4,,lisrti represented a ratio of 827 7 per 1,000, as ,;114,, t 689 Germans; the pexceutage of con- -46-Z4Y non-effe3tive through sickness was oth"° in our own case, against 10.6 in Vie A„„mer • the British death-rate was 9.05 per : against 2.4 of Germans. Tme :4eath-rete of infants under one year old has .creased from 149 per 1,000 in the period 1871.-80 „7 163 per 1,000 in 1900; there has been an „„crease* of deaths owing to congenital defects of -`"-Iu per cent. in thirty years; and the deaths from Premature childbirth have increased by 300 per Cent in the last fifty years. RETIREMENT OF Mn. JOFIN MRA.DOWCROFT.

We understand that Mr. John Mea.dowcroft will shortly retire from the position of Runcorn agent of the Bridgewater Department of the Manchester Ship Canal Company. Mr. Meadowcroft has been connected with the Bridgewater undertakings for 45 years, and has held the position of agent under the Ship Canal Company for 25 years. He will retain the secretary- ship of the Upper Alersey Navigation Commissioners, and be succeeded in the agency of the Bridgewater undertaking by Mr. Evans of Duke's Dock, Liverpool.

ALLEGED INFRINGEMENT OF THE LAW.

At the Runcorn 'Petty Sessions on Monday before Messrs. A. R. Norman (in the chair) and W. Timmins, Messrs. T. Rigby and Sons, flour and corn mer Chants, Frodsham, were summoned for running a locomotive on the highway and failing to employ a sullicient number of meu to work it.—Mr. Fletcher, of Northwich, appared for the defendants and' pleaded not guilty. The evidence showed that Sergeant Bennett saw a man named Jones, employed by the defendants,on the Chester road on the 24th March, in charge of a locomotive drawing a light waggon. He was the only person in charge, the weight being 2 tons 18cwt. It did not con- sume its own smoke and blew off steam.—The defence contended that the locomotive satisfied the require- ments of the Locomotive Act of 1896. They were being used all over the country and no complaints had hitherto been made. The engine did • consume its own smoke,being specially constructed to do so.—The bench dismissed the case.

===== 0002601_19030501_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
NEWTON DIVISION CONSERVATIVE ASSOCIATION.

The annual meeting of this Association was held in the Town Hall Newton-le-Willows on Saturday after- noon. There was a large attendance of delegates from various districts in the divisiOn. Lord Newton presided and was supported by Colonel Pilkington M.P., 'Messrs, C. B. F. Borron, T. J. Gillespie, R. Stone, S. S. Brown, W. Collingwood, Aldermen Francomb, and Sutton, Messrs Mr. W. Holland Owen (hon. secretary), J. Robinson, and W. A. Mawdsley (registration agent). On the motion of Mr. W. Holland Owen, seconded by Mr. R. Stone, the following vote of condolence was passed in silence: "That this the annual meeting of the Newton Division Conservative Association desires to place on record the valuable service rendered by the late William Pilkington,Esq., J.P., D.L., to the Conser- vative party generally,and especially &wing the time he was chairman of the South-West Lancashire,district and latterly as chairman of the St. Helens polling district of the Newton Division, and feel that his death is a great loss to the Conservative and Unionist cause, and sincerely sympathize with the family in their sad bereavement."

Lord Newton delivered a lengthy speec'? dealing with current events,and had to admit that the Unionist party was in a somewhat weaker position than it was 12 months ago. If any party had gained at all in strength it was in his opinion the so-culled Labour party. The Irish Land Bill was a complicated thing and it was difficult to pronounce an opiu- ion off hand. He touched upon the Bagdad Railway question which was one that interested him very much, as he happened to be one of very few per- sons in this country who had travelled on that railway so far as it had been completed. He did not believe that Germany had any serious aims in that part of the world. The aim of the German Government was to develop Germany in Europe and South Africa. Yet it was proposed that we should find the money and Ger- many should retain the management of the railway in their hands for their own purposes. Looking at the whole circumstances of the case he thought we were very well out of the proposed bargain. With regard to the Budget he expressed himself as opposed to the abolition of the corn tax.

Colonel R. Pilkington, M.P., gave a lengthy speech in support of a resolution of confidence in the Govern- ment. The criticism of independent Unionist members with regard to the army, in his opinion, had been most undeserved. He held that Mr. Brodrick was a veryable Secretary for War, who was doing his utmost to remedy the defects of the army system. In spite of all that was said about extravagant expenditure on the army, the percentage of expenditure on the army was higher in 1863 and in 1864, when the army was on a peace footing, than it was now. He balieved that difficulties would vanish when the party got into thorough working order under their new captain, and that at the next general election their majority would be found as great as it was at present. (Applause.) The resolution was adopted, and a vote of confidence in Colonel R. Pilkington was passed.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

LB CORRESPOND ENT].

The Earlestown May Queen festival which is put down for the 30th of the present month promises to be more picturesque than any of its predecessors. The committee have this year succeeded in obtaining the patronage of Lord Darby, Lord Newton, Colonel Pilk- ington, M.P., and other notabilities, and if the whole of the gentlemen whose names appear on the poster, show their sympathy in a practical manner, the financial suc- cess of the undertaking is assured. All that is needed to secure the attendance of the general public in large numbers, is fine weather, for upon this the success of all out door affairs of this kind depend.

I wonder when the District Council will be able to see their way to give us water pure and bright. To me it is miraculous some epidemic does not break out, because of the dirty stite of t 4 crystal (?) fluid, it looks more like dirty beef tea than water, and yet they are constantly flushing the pipes to no purpose. Many peop.le seem to think it is polluted by the water from Newton lake, and if the smell is anything to go by, they are not very far wrong. Mr. Clarke promised his con- stituents a pure and plentiful supply of water, and per- haps when he has been able to accustom himself to his surroundings, they will get it.

RUNCORN FOOTBALL CLUB. Review of the Season's Play.

Now that the oval is all but displaced by the bat and ball it is not inappropriate to briefly review the suc- cesses and failures wnich have fallen to the lot of the wearers of the myrtle green. On the whole,the season has been well up to the average, and there can be no doubt that if Runcorn could have won the coveted smiles of Dame Fortune—as some clubs have the knack of doing—local football enthusiasts would have been occupying a very proud position indeed to-day. The traditions of the club have been well and worthily main- tained and the Linnets have shown the opponents how to play "football," not by vulgar use of brute strength, but by scientific tactics which have wnirthe admiration of the spectators even when they have not won points. I The season was inaugurated by the Linnets meeting Hunslet, it being the first match where the new rule of the scrummage instead of the punt out was played. At that time it was confidently anticipated .that the rule would favour the locals to the discomfiture af their opponents, but as a matter of fact, other clubs have really benefited much more by the adoption oft he rule than Runcorn. The second match may fittingly be termed the local "Derby" Runcorn and Widnee. meeting each.other at Irwell-laue where the former registered a win after an exciting and vigorous game by ten points to nothing. 'Our doughty opponents Bradford, were next taken on at Park-avenue, the Runcorn forwards for once excelling themselves making a draw of two points each, the visitors having the best -of the game. Huddersfield were next thrashed, after which the Myrtles visited Warrington, where a most exciting game terminated in a draw—a just finish to a hard fought game. The next team to visit Irwell-lane were Brighouse Rangers where the home contingent were given such a fight as they have not had for many years. The visitors managed early on to cross the line and it was not until the last few minutes of the game that the home players, impelled by the frantic implorings of the spectators,. raced over and scored thrice.lA poor exhibition with the Saintly Saints of St. Helens terminated in a draw. After this the Myrtles got back to their usual form by castigating Swinton in no half hearted manner by thirteen 'points to two the burly forwards of the "Lions" completely falling away before the smart tactics of the Runcorn backs. It wa.s in this .game that Richardson excelled hitnself4gaining one of the most meritorious tries of the season. Salford were next on the list away, and, according to an eye witness, the visitors were winning up to thirty seconds of the finish, when Bell burst away, and, outwitting the redoubtable Houghton scored at the corner, thus pulling the match out of the fire by ten points to six. This match ititroduced to the football world one of the finest centre three-quarters Runcorn have ever possessed, Harry Price, who scored Runcorn's initial try. The shift of scenery beings on the stage that ever nuttnoraba match which Runcorn supporters and the North of England generally will always remember as being one in which the "slow to wrath " Cheshire town were robbed of a victory owing to the impartial (?) decisions of the one placed in authority to hold at a dead level the scales of justice. I refer to Halifax; and, therefore, further comment is needless. A typical game of West Lancashire football was wit- neased when Leigh were taken on—a pbintless draw be- ing the result. Hull were next encountered—but not at Trwell-lane—Runcorn having been forced to play.three home matches at Birkenhead. Smarting under the sentence of punishment,the Linnets lost by three points to seven. Frost now intervening, matches were post- poned only to be inconveniently played at the fag end of the season. The next m itch saw Runcorn's heavy hand smiting Hull Kingston Rovers to the tune Of twenty-one points to nothing. A depleted team was than taken to Batley viiitch resulted in a bad defeat by thirteen points to nothing—an unusual thing for Run- corn's defence to be pierced three times. Up to this point Runcorn had not won a single match away, but at Wigan the tide turned, Moran being introduced for the first time and scoring a remarkable try, the visitors winning by three points to two. 'rhe beginning of the year saw Runcorn visiting Widnes, where the game was played in a perfect blizzard. The wind in the first hallhelpedthe home team considerably, a try being scored which Runcorn could not wipe out. On January 10th Bradford were entertained, the match being one of the most exciting ever seen on the Runcorn ground. At half-time Bradford were leading by seven points to two, and then further increased their lead by two points. Hounded on by their supporters. the home aontingent made a desperate attempt to draw level, and general satisfaction reigned when Walker gained a fine try from a cross kick. Hardly had the game been resumed when Price passed out to Moran who scored within three minutes of the finish. Farmer added the necessary points. Even then Runcorn had not finished, for on the stroke Richardson dashed through and completed the rout of the Yorkshiremen by fifteen points to nine. The Wirepullers of Warring- ton were the next to come to Runcorn, where their defence and attack fell completely to pieces. Naturally the visitors were disappointed, but the Myrtles were irresistable. and won by 18 points to nothing—the biggest defeat Warrington have had this season. Points were then sought at Brighouse, but the Runcora team was sent to the right about by eight points to three. Riincorn, however, triumphed over Huddersfield by three points to nothing, and Wigan to the tune of nine- teen points to nothing. It seemed, indeed, that nothing could withstand their victorious march, I and high hopes were entertained that the champions of , Cheshire would win the Northern League Cup. The Cup Ties next claimed attention, Runcorn having very lucky draws at home. Widnes were the opponents in the first round, and after a vigorous—too vigorous— they were defeated by seven points too two. Drawing ; with Birkenhead in the second round the match was played in a storm of rain the finish witnessing the triumph of Runcorn by eighteen points to nothing. Ma rypo rt were the next to be taken on,the locals winning .easily by seventeen points to three. The next round in which Runcorn thought they could avenge their former disastrous meeting, was with Halifax. The game was. played in the characteristic Yorkshire manner, and re-, suited in the downfall of Runcorn's high hopes. Shortly before , time Hadwi. marked about I thirty yards from the posts, Little kicking a goal thus giving the visitors the winning points. This , quenched Runcorn's light for the Northern Union Cup, but they determined to win the League Championship. ; Swinton and Salford were defeated by the hard work of the rear division, but Halifax again blocked the way and though Runcorn were certain they could pull off the game they were defeated at Hanson-lane after having the best of the game by eight points. This, practically, I knocked the locals out of the League, yet visiting Old- ham in a half-hearted fashion they lost by three points to nil—the score being indicative of the merits of the two teams—as will be seen on referring to the League! table. An Easter tour ended in a miserable fiasco, all the games being lost. Batley redeemed Runcorn's I credit by permitting themselves to be beaten ty eighteen points to eight. Determined to show a bold front to the last, Hunslet were ;knooked out by nine points to six, Price scoring ! a try in methods a la association, dribbling three parts of the field. The season terminated by defeating Wigan by eight points to three. The works competitions have proved very successful, Highfleld winning the medals by knocking Cast ner's out in the final by two tries to one. There is no doubt that next season Runcorn's sun—in spite of S. Houghton's rumoured retirement— will shine out as refulgently as ever.

Barnton conducted and Miss Toombs presided at the organ. The collections which were in aid of the Sunday School funds totalled £7O. BARLESTOWN BOOKMAKERS RAIDED.

I A number of charges of obstructing the footway at Earlestown by bookmakers were heard on Thursday morning at the Newton-le-Willews Polise Court.—Mr. Browta aated that obstruction through street-betting at Earlestown had become such an intolerable nuisance that the Earlestown Co-operative Society had placed their premises at the use of the police for the purpose of watching defendants. The offences took place on April 17th and 18th, and at one timethere would be as many as fifty or sixty people standing on the feotwa.y for five minutes at a stretch, whilst at times the whole street was blocked by people betting with defendants. —Matthew Seton was fined 20s. and costs in two cases, Unsworth tbe same penalty in two cases; Frank Dale and Edward Dale 20s. and costs in one case respectively; whilst the charge against Walter Water- man mas dismissed, defendant calling evidence that he was not at the place at the time stated by the police.— Mr. Knowles asked the Bench to state a case in respect to Setou's convictions, but the magistrates fdeclined the application.

===== 0002601_19030501_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
EARLESTOWN MAY QUEEN. FESTIVAL

The forthcoming display which is to take place on Saturday, May 30th, is expected to excel all previous efforts in this direction, and given fine weather, there should be a record crowd. Last year, which is the first venture brought to the funds of St. John's Church enlargement project, a veryconsiderable amount, but it is hoped this will be exceeded this year. If the ener- gies of the Committee go for anything, those who go to the trouble of pkying a visit to Earlestown will certainly be amply reward. The idea of holding the festival on the Market Ground really originated with Mr. Kilmister, the able secretary, and his wisdom as the result showed, was not out of place. The labour in pre- paring the ground was enough to daunt any ordinary person, but not Mr. Kilmister, who infected the other members of the Committee with his enthusiasm with the result that the aPparently insurmountable difficulties and the criticisms of those who were adverse to it, were wiped aside, and the financial result fully repaid all the efforts put forth. The same untiring energy is being displayed on this occasion, and no doubt will receive the reward it deserves.

IDENPENDENT ORDER OF RECHABITES. -- WaPrington District, No 4.

The quarterly meeting of this Society was held on Saturday last .at the Brunswick-road Mission IEII, Earlestown. The proceedings were presided over by Bro. Walker, D.D.C.R., in the absence of Bro. .1. Hucklleston D.C.R., through indisposition, when repre- sentatives from every tent in the district reported them- selves. The D.S. presentee his report from which it appeared that there are 19 tents lathe district with a Membership of 1189 with a nett increase for the quarter of 25. Total amount of funds £3,750. Value of property belonging to the district £3,150. A new Welsh., tent was aboht to be openedat Ashton and he hoped before long to be able to report that one had been opened at Upholland. Resolved that the report of the D.S.be accepted as read.. The District Auditors reported that they had examined All books and vouchers which were correct and great credit was due to the D.S. upon the way in which the accounts were kept. Bro. J. Wright, J.D.S., in presenting his report said - the quarter had been one of success. There had been an increase of juveniles of 52 after deducting 4 who had been transferred to the adult tents. AThe total juvenile membership was 011. The financial increase after pay- ing the death claim was .£l7 10s. 014. total funds £230 7s. 6i-d. New juvenile tents were about to be forme at Lowton and Padgate, and he hoped before the present year expired the membership would be 1,060. He was not asking for impossibilities, for considering how that success had attended their efforts in the past by a little more exertion their object would beachieved, and the Order Challenge Shield would find its way to Warrington District for the greatest increase in juvenile membership during the year. A resolution was passed denouncing the action of the Prime Minister in seeking to prejudice the unfettered discretion of Justices and inciting Quarter Sessions to reverse the decision of Licensing Magistrates ' • also protesting against any attempt being made by Parliament to create vested interest in licences or to provide compensation in cases where in the exercise of their discretionary powers, magistrates- refuse to renew such licences and that this meeting appealsto all temperance and social reformers to resist every effort to provide such compensation or to limit the power of licencinn.b justices. That copies of the resolution be forwarded to the following Parlia- mentary Representatives, Robert Pierpoint Esq., M.P., for the County Borough of ‘Varringten, Colonel R. Pil- kington, M.P., for Newton Division and Colonel-Blun- dell M.P., for lace Division. After all the business had been disposed of, Bro. Baxendale was called upon to present to Bro. Horne a beautiful framed emblem of the Order in recognition of his services to the District, he having passed through the various chairs and completed his term of office. In making the presentation Bro. Baxendale expressed him- self as being pleased to have the honour conferred upon him. Bro. Horne had been an. honest and earnest worker during his term, he was a steady going man, and one , who it was a pleasure to work with. He had helped to. ' form the Juvenile Council, and had been appointed a trustee. He hoped he would accept the present with the best wishes of the members• present as a slight acknowledgment of his past services. 1 Bro. Horne thanked Bro.Baxeudale and the District Council for the present adding that he was proud to be the recipient of such a token of their goodwill towards him. It would find a prominent place in his home and whenever he looked upon it he would be reminded of their good wishes and the many happy hours spent with them. Bro. Hall then responded to the invitation to present o the district secretary Bro. T. Winstanley a valuable roll-top secretaire for his services as Clerk of Works daring the erection of property at Earlestown. In asking him to accept the present he said he had not, during his long connection with the Rechabites extending over 40 years seen a more suitable present offered to anyone. Bro. Winstanley was well worthy of it. When he took over the office some five years ago they had only five tents,now they had eleven. They might say he had not formed them, but he had been mainly instrumental in the formation of them by his industry and zeal. The work which he had done in connection with the property they would never forget. The speaker trusted Mr. Winstanley would live long to enjoy their confidence and that he might continue to exhibit the same energy and spirit which had char- acterised his work in the past. Bro. Winstanley in reply, said it was extremely grati- fying to him to receive such a gift as this from their hands. It was the friendly feeling which had always been extended towards him by all,that made it pleasant to labour amongst them. He accepted the present, not because of its pecuniary value, but because of the spirit in which it had been given. ,He thanked them very heartily for it and assured them that if spared he should continue to work for the interests .of the district and cause. Bros. E. Walkden and Massey also spoke in terms of praise of Bros. Home and Winstanley. In the evening a concert was given by various male and female members from Ashton, Bryan and Warring- ton.' Bro. J. Shaw of Warrington 'Occupied the chair. Bro. D. Sutton as humorist excelled himself and re- ceived numerous encores. Durin,, the evening Bro.. Wright in a forcible address on the juvenile questiomt called upon all members and friends to enroll their children so that they might be reared in principles of Rechabitism. It is hoped that the meeting may give a fillip to the local tents both juvenile and adult.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football. By " Ovalist."

The football season is once more at an end and the last kick was given to the ban in Warrington on Wed- nesday evening, when the final tie in the Works Com- petition was played at Wilderspool before a fine crowd of about 4,001 J. Such support as this testifies not only to the firm hold the game has on the Warrington public, but to the popularity of the competition between the local works' teams. Those present,unless they were Supporters of the defeated team, would not be disappointed, for the football dig-played was of dis- tinctly good class, consideriug that the exponents were novices. Crosfield's and Rylands' were the con- testants and they proved well-matched, though the score was 10 points to 3 points in favour of the soap-makers at . the close. Rylands' played shockingly for two-thirds of the game or , such a score would never have been run up. Later on they rallied well and obtained the best try of the evening. Their fault lay chiefly with the half backs, who, until late on, seemed afraid to trust their three quarters with the ball. Crosifeld's on the contrary essayed the open game and profited by it. They were meritorious winners and deserved the handsome gold medals which Messrs. Eustance and Co., have supplied to the order of the Football Club. The runners-up received silver medals with a gold centre and have reason to be well satisfied whim their reward. I wish them better luck next time, for unlike -Crosfield's they have never woe the competi- tion outright yet.

Tile Warrington men played Hu ldersfield for the concluding .contest of the season and finished up with a creditable win, though the Fartowners pushed them hard. Fish and Harris were the scorers for the home side. This victory givol Warrington a creditable position in thg League, as will be seen from another column, which contains my annual review of the season.

Saturday's match was rather a tame affair. Brilli- ant individual efforts were made by Harris and Fish who scored the tries, but there was a. lack of com- bination on both sides and neither team distinguished itself by dashing play. This may perhaps be accounted for by the fact that there was very little at stake in this the closing match of the season. The;heat, too, was rather oppressive and 'militated against either side playing in their most robust style. The Warrington forwards, as usual, had the worst of the argument with eheir opponents, who heeled out of the scrimmage about twice as often as the War- rington vanguard. Holroycl and Dewhurst were given plenty_ of opportunities to get the back machinery into operation, but many chances were lost owing to the passing being either too slow or ill-timed. The best Combined movement of the visiting backs was i that which led to Wimpenny scoring n the second half.

Of the individual players, Harris and Fish stand out far.and away above the rest. Harris played up to his Bradford reputation and the try which he gained must rank as one of his finest performances. The manner in which he brushed away his opp3nents and cmtinued his career for the -line An a dogged zig-zag manner was refreshing to witness, deserved the appreciation which all the spe- tutors ungrudgingly bestowed. In the second half Fish emulated Harris's example by making an individual effort which was equally meritorious. It, will be remembered that at Hufliers- field Deere got the better of Fish and sc3red two tries, On Saturday, Fish turned the tables on his opponent and completely outclassed him . at. all pints in the, game. -Fish did not succeed in registering two tries but his one try was obtained in masterly style. Getting 'possession as the result of a faulty piss, Fish raced away and kicking the ball over Kitchen's head, he regained possession and crossed the line. One saterical friend was heard to shout" 0 Deere! " but the Huddersfield right-wing man alth mgh chagrined seemed to be content with his _well-won laurels at Huddersfield.

Hallam did not play no to th top oflais form and on the afternoon's play Kitcheui was the better man. Isherwood and Dickenson also showA a fallincr,-off in their play but this is excusable in the last matelt of the season and they will all be readily forgiv.m if they return to their b?st f min by S3ptemb3r next.

Cricket.

The capital form displayed by the Warringtonians in the opening match was maintained on Saturday; when Newton-le-Willows were easily accounted for on their own ground. Batting first, the visitors com- piled the substantial, total of 129, to which Newton revonded with only 45. Warrington had the assist- ance of J. W. Bibbv, the ponular captain, who ren- dered most valuable service with the ball, being responsible for 5 wickets at the small cost of 12 runs. He must be complimented upon this creditable achievement.

Lynes, the Warrington professional, proved him- self a batsman of no mean ability, and showed that in this department as well as regards bowling he is a decided acquisition to the eleven. He knocked up just as many runs as the combined efforts of the Newton men realised-45. He batted in splendid style, not one faulty stroke marring his exhibition. and hit no fewer .than eight boundary strokes. W. M. Silcock was also prominent with a fine battingdis- play, his aggregate being 21. W. H. Pognore knocked up 19 and J. A. Briggs 10.

Not a single Newton player attained double tig,nres, and all experienced difficulty in dealing with the balls sent down by the visitors, and particularly lw Mr. Bibby who maintained a' capital length and made the ball turn quickly from the pitch. T. Williams was th,e top snorer, his efforts being productive of 9 runs, whilst H. Rylance came next with 5. The re- sult of the engagement was very gratifying to the Warrington supporters and a successful season is hoped for.
