PROTECTING THE CHILDREN.

THE WORK OF THE N.S.P.C.C. IN WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT.

Tnteresting details of the useful work ctirried on by the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children in the Warrington .district were forthcoming the annual meeting of the Warrington branch which was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall on Thursdiy afternoon. Mr. F. A. Frost presided over a large attendance which included Colonel • Buckton, Councillor Bennett. Mr. McGregor, Mr. %V. D. Jeans, Jun. ( secretary), and many ladies. - The SECRETARY real letters of -ap4ogy for non- attendance from the Mayor, Mr. R. Pierpoint M.P., Mr. Roger Parr, Cal. Cornw ill Legh, Mr. Sutton Timmis,Mr. T. Birchall. J.P., Mr. W. Long, Mr. John White, Mrs. Blecklv, and said many others had been received. The *Cattirm in said the letters numbered about sixty or seventy.

THE ANNUAL REPORT,

StJGGEiTED NEW DISTRICT OR ADDITIONAL

INSPECTOR.

The CITAIRILAN said they had met to review the ,general work of the braach during the past year, which he thought they would find had on the vhole been a highly satisfactory one. They.hopd to be able to do still better in the future as there was plenty of room for improvement. . S'ECRE r.k RY elevesth tunnel re?ort of the Committee which read as follows :—The com- mittee have to report a considerable decrease in the number of casas dealt with during the year ended 31st March, 1903, The' total number of cases investigated ,of this period has beea 313 affecting 952 children of whom 463 were boys and 190 were girls, In 213 'cases warning w3re given, 39 were pr mecitted, 9 were other- wise dealt with, 8 were dropped, and I was on the 31st. March pending. The prosecatioas (39) which numbered one more than last year resulted in the conviction of 31 persons who were sent to prison for various tips aggregating 7 years and 10 months. Twelve other per- sons were censured and their cases adjourned to give them a chance of improvement, and two ctses were withdrawn owing to the serious illness of the offenders. 258 of the cases were neglect and starvation, 23 ill-treatment. 3 exnasure, 5 causing to beg, 5 immoral surroundings, 2 abmd mment, and 2 criminal assault. Upwards of 1,090 sup zrvision visits were ptid during the year. and assistance in the shape of food, bedding, coal andclothin,_ff has in m eyes been provided for families who weTe in immediate mud. There are at present about 500 families in the dOitrict under the Society's observation. - The diminution in the number of cases has been brought about partly by a.continued improvement in the town :of Warrington, and partly by an alteration which has hesivnirle in the district covered by the branch, the town of Widnes, which formerly pro- vided a great deal of the Inspector's work having been taken from this district and added to that of the Liverpool outer branch. This alteration took effect from the month of August 1902. It had become urgent to make some change in this way, as the work in the districts had become heavier then one Inspector could manage. It should he mentioned that the Inspector of the Liverpool outer district has in consequence of the change,begun to find his work beyond him, and there is now talk of either creating a new district to cover Widnes. Runcorn and Frodshon• or of employing an ad- ditional Inspector either at Liverpool or here to assist in that neighbourhood. The conimittee are pleased to be able to report a sur_mcessf ma financial year. The activity of Mrs. Frost and the ladies who have assisted her has resulted in an increased income, and there is a credit balance of .£ll 10s. The annual • ball was also, thanks to Mrs. Frost amid the other ladies acting with her on the committee, unusually successful, the large sum of £39 17s. M. be- ing realized thereby. Mks Broadbent continues to take an active interest in the branch of the Children's League of Pity, and the Society is indebted to her and the league for a most useful sum of money collected in that way. Inspector Hackett continues to give every satisfaction and the committee consider that the general [improvement in the district is to a great extent the result of his energetic work. The police in every part 'of the district are also very good in assisting the Society where their assistance is needed. In War. rington especially the Chief Constable and the forse generally have from time to time given most value- able help. So also have many of the school attendance ' officers and school masters and mistresses. Thanks are also due to the Cheshire Needlework Guild (through the kind offices of Lady Brooke), and the several ladles for gifts of clothing, bedding etc., which are always ac- ceptable. The Inspector has always , cases in hand where such things are very badly needed and will be grateful at all times to any ladies for worn-out cloth- ing, old bedding, blankets, etc."

_ Colonel Buci•ox moved the adoption of the report. He said he thought everyone present knew the objects of the Society and the work taken in hand, and was very keenly desirous that it should he carried out as thoroughly as possible. The work of the Inspector had not hen as heavy this year, as part of the district had been cut off, but the district that Was left seemed to have provided an enormous amount of work. They could not help feeling what a p;ty it was that so much of such work had to be done. Before the branch was started people seemed co think there would be nothing for it to do. but it has been found that there was plenty of work. The branch had been in operation for ten years and had done an immense amount of work in pro- tecting the children. Of course the work entailed financial responsibility, and the actual resnonsibility was undertaken by Mr. Waugh and the officials at head quarters, but still it was the duty of the branch to try and provide for its own expenses. He was pleased to say that the Warrington branch appeared to be doing very well.

Mr. MCGRAIGOR seconded, and said he thought the branch was moving on a little better than it was some time ago. The amount of work left to be done after their ten years missionary work in the neighbourhood was certainly very considerable. He thought, however, that the figures showed an improvement as the propor- tion of cases of cruelty seemed to be diminishing. The report was unanimously adopted. Mr T T WArrs, of Northwich, delivered an interest- ing address.

Sir Gilbert. Greenall, Bart, was re-elected President. of the Branch.

HOLIDAYS TN NORTII WA Ls.—North Wales and the various watering places to be found along the coasts have always been popular to Lancashire and Cheshire people, not only on account of the natural beauties of almost any particular district that may be named, but also by reason of the facilities which are placed at the disposal of those who desire to make a closer acquaint- ance with any of the interesting localities indicated. Madame .Albani's Visit to Warrington.

The visit of Madame Albani who will appear at Mr. R.' H. Pearson's special grand concert on Tuesday May 12th, in the Parr Hall, is .exciting much local interest. The world-famous queen of song has only just returned from a concert tour in Canada, where she and her party have met with nothing but success. The artistes who will come with her to Warrington are all of the highest class, and the opportunity of hearing so famous a vocalist—an opportunity, by the. *4, which is not likely to occur again in the immed- iate future--should be sufficient to fill the hall. The distinguished violinist, Madame Beatrice Langley, is well known, and Miss Adela Verne is recognised as the best lady pianiste of the day. Miss Muriel Foster moreover, holds a very prominent place in the list of leading contraltos, and has during the last few months been fulfilling engagements in Moscow,and in addition, that ever popular veteran, Mr. Santley, whose niche amongst the baritones will be very hard to fill, will also sing at this memorable concert, and c.)mpletes a concert party that cannot fail to give the utmost. pleasure and satisfaction.

N

IMPORTANT TO ALL.

Every person's future is, to a certain extent, in his own hand. Many a future which might have been brilli- ant has been darkened by lack of the spirit of self-help. Many a home, which might have been gladdened by life and health, and comfort, has been saddened by poverty, illness, and death, in many cases wholly preventible. The spring and summer months are, from a sanitary point of view, among the most important of the year. Due care may now mean a healthy and happy summer and autumn' and robust constitution to meet the trials of next winter. Neglect may now mean a joyless summer, a trying autumn, and a broken constitution to succumb under the attacks of any exceptional trial. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

DISORDERLY PASSENGER ON A TRAMCAR.-LADIES

COMPELLED TO LEAVE.

Joseph Waller, a foreman bricklayer residing in Chapel-lane. Sankey was charged at the Warrington Police Court on Monday,before Mr. F. Monks and other magistrates, with wilfully interfering with the comfort of passengers on a Corporation tramcar on Saturday,April 18th. He pleaded 'guilty.—Mr. Stanley Wilson, deputy town clerk, prosecutedon behalf of the Corpora- tion, and said that the defendant was a passenger on the 4.55 car from Wilderspool on the date named. When the car arrived at St. James's Church, Checker Davenport went inside the car to examine the tickets and noticed the defendant with his head in his hands,npparently asleep, and his hat Was on the floor. After the checker had examined the tickets he was passing the defendant, when the latter jumped up, seized his hat, and started flourishing it about in front of the passengers. One gentleman had his hat knocked off, and the defen- dant also attempted to strike the conductor. Defendant used bad language and his conduct was so bad that all the ladies on the car got out, and one of the other passengers assisted the checker to hold the defendant down until the car reached Bridge Foot, when the ser- vices of a constable were obtained. Defendant's con- duct throughout the journey was most disgraceful. According to alitatement Imade to one of the witnesses the defenant evidently thought he was in South Africa snd that would probably account for his mad conduct. He was under the influence of drink, and when at the Police Station he used very bad language, and the police had to threaten to put him in the cells. He understood however, that the defendant was a hard-workingman, and had never been in court before, and he was sorry he had to prosecute a person of that character. Thq Cor- poration were, however, bound to protect the public. D3fendant was under the influence of drink— The Chief Constable said defendant was like a madman :at the Police Station, and it was only in consideration .of the feat that he was a locAil resident that he was bailed out.—Conductor Timperley and Checker Daven- port gave evidence.—Defendant said he had been up several nights through the illness of his child, and had also been troubled with his head. He hardly knew what he was doing. He very seldom took drink.—Mr. Hallard, clerk of works at the Sankey Sanatorium, said the defendant had worked under him for about two years, and he had never known him speak a wrong word durinc, the whole of the time. He could not under- stand doing such a thing, as his conducthad always been of a most exemplary character, and his language to himself (Mr. Hallard) and the men was beyond re- proach. He had never seen him take drink.—The Chairman (Mr. F. Monks), said that Mr. Hallard's good word had helped the defendant, and the Bench had de- cided not to record a conviction.—Defendant must pay the costs and contribute 10s. to the poor box, and the ease would be dismissed.

THE ART OF SWIMMING. Annual Meeting of the Warrington Swimming Club.

Evidence was afforded of the near approach of the swimming season, when the members of the Warrington Swimming Club met on Monday evening for the pur- pose of appointing their officers and preparing for the polo matohes. The meeting was presided over by Mr. F. Garside, and it was held in one of the new rooms at the Baths.

===== 0002601_19030501_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BALANCE SHEET

The balance sheet, which had been audited by Messrs. R. T. Harding and F. Garside, was presented. It showed that the income, including a balance brought forws.rd of £9 4s. lOid., amounted to £35 17s. 14d. The members' subscriptions amounted to £l4 135., and the profit on gala was £1 16s. 4d. After meeting all expenditure there was a balance carried forward of £9 4s. 2id.,being Bd. less than the balance in hand at the beginning of the year. Mr. GROUNDS stated that considering the past year was an unfavourable one, the balance sheet was very satisfactory. The first polo team did not win a match, their best performance being a draw. Mr. J. GRAY said that £l4 had beeti given in prizes which was equal to the members' subsoriptions. The balance sheet was adopted on the proposition of Mr. A. ASHTON, seconded by Mr. W. SUTTON.

===== 0002601_19030501_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
VOTES OF THANKS.

Mr. WOOD proposed a vote of-thanks to the Town Hall, Parks and. Baths Committee for concessions made at galas and polo matches. The CITAIRM AN' seconded, and thought perhaps the sommittee might supplement the concessions by pro- iding a few seats. (Hear, hear.) _ . .

The- vote was carried as was also a vote of thanks to the press.

PRESIDENT AND VICE-PRESIDENTS.

It was unanimously Lgreed to RA the Mayor (Mr. J. C. Parr) to again .be president of the club.

The Vice-Presidents were re-elected. CAPTAIN AND OFFICERS OF THE CLUB,

The CFIAIRMIN said the next business was the ap- pointment of a captain of the club. Mr. Thomas Locker was the'captain last year. . _ _

Mr. ARTHUR ASHTON remarked that he did not see the necessity of appointing a captain of the club, and asked what the duties of that officer were.

The CHAIRMAN stated that it was an honorary posi- tion.

Mr. ASHTON thought if there was any honour it ought to be given to the captain of the polo team, who was supposed to do the work and be responsible for the team.

The CHAIRMAN further pointed out that the captain of the club represented the whole body of members apart from the team. _ _

Mr. ASHTON considered he should be a playing mem- ber, but withdrew his suggestion. ' Mr. T. LOCKER was then unanimously re-elected captain of the club. . Mr. A. Boscow (last year's sub-captain)was appointed captain of the polo team and Mr. Arthur Ashton. sub- captain. It was decided that the general committee should elect a captain for the second team at a later period: Mr. C. Wood was again appointed hon.treasurer, and Messrs. G. Grounds and W. Ashton were elected joint hon. secretaries, the latter succeeding Mr. J. Gray, who did not wish to ' stand again. Mr. G. Grounds was voted deletate on the Northern Counties, Lancashire County, and Liverpoolazid District Water Polo Associa- tions. Messrs. R. T. Harding- and F. Garside were re- elected hon. auditors. The following were elected on the committee :---Messrs. E. Draper, A. Boscow, W. Sutton, A. Ashton, C. Ashton, R. T. Harding, S. J. Bea- man, R. Allen, C. Turner. J. Peers, J. Gray, and F. Gar- side. It was suggested that the rules be revised by the committee, printed, and supplied to each member.

CONDEMNED TO DE4TH.

Pathetic Scene at Walthamstow.

"I don't think your mother will be here in the morn- ing. You had better telegraph to your father." The tone was earnest. The girl who listened burst into tears, as the doctor went softly downstairs and kft the house. wr Her mother, then, was dying !• Behind that dumb, white-painted door, a tragedy was being enacted. Lack- ing the energy to send a telegram to the absent husband —a well-known electrical engineer, then working on one of the Co--operative Cruising Company's fine boats—the girl slipped in and sat beside the patient.

"I have been wondering," said the sufferer,—Mrs. Maddeaux was her name; of 53, Northcote-road, Wel- thamstow—" whether Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people might not do me good. I hear such wonderful things of them." The suggestion was acted upon, and Mrs. Maddeaux lived to tell the story, a strong and healthy woman, snatched from the very grave. "I am." she said, "the mother of sixteen children, and had been suffering from peritonitis. It began by my feeling a little run down, but I soon found that I could not eat anything without suffering great pain, which caused the stomach to distend, and agonising pains to set in. The least movement caused me pain; I grew light-headed, and want of sleep made me look like an old woman dragged to pieces. Even the short flight of stairs to the flrst,floor I had to climb up by means of the balustrade, resting every third or fourth stair. Doctors said I was gradually sinking into the grave. Of course, I had the very best medical advice. "When I was given up. I tried Dr. Williams' pink pills as a last hope. They saved my life. Strength returned. I gradually began to gain flesh and go about the house; the terrible depression disappeared. lam completely restored to health, and feel stronger than I ever felt in my life." Strength and the saving of life are what Dr. Williams' pink pills offer to the ueak. Consumptives, wasting away from loss of blood, find in them new blood and new hope. St. Vitus' dance, anasmia, paralysis, rheumatism, fits, kidney disease, and all the ills that make woman's life a • burden have been cured by the genuine pills. Sold at two shillings and ninepence a box; to be had of local chemists, for if they bear the full name they will always be genuine.

The Examiner, Friday, May 1, 1903.

cartoon, the Chancellor of the Exchequer had prepared a soft place for them to fall upon. (Laughter.) They hoped their fall would ho soon. He supposed it would be like the Irishman's description who said there wasn't much in the fall but in the sudden stop at the bottom. (Laughter.) In a conversation with a Tory in that division, he was surprised to hear that Tory describe the present Governmentas a Govern- ment of "sops." They " sopped " the publicans' the clergy, the landlords, and now they were coming back to"pop" the landlords of Ireland and poor old EnglAnd in the way of the Budget. Mention had been made of the mess the Government had got into through mis- management. There was no doubt that the man at the head of affairs, according to that most amiable Conser- vative, the Hon. George Kenyon, was not gifted with sufficient discretion to find out the right men to fill the right places. Mr. Balfour certainly ought not to be where he was, but at the head of the Established Church. He had made some excellent appointments of bishops— (laughter)—but great mistakes in the selection of men to fill the Government offices. (Hear, hear.) They trusted he would put a man in the late Mr. Hanburv's place who would be able to fill it as well as Mr. fan- bury had done. Referring to the Education Act, Mr. Jones pointed out what was. being done in North • Wales. Through the instrumentality of Mr. Lloyd George the question of levying the rate had been deferred. It had decided to institute an inquiry into the sanitary condition of the voluntary schools before taking over the manage- ment under the new Bill. If any of them had read Mr. Winston Churchill's to the Conservative Association they would be perfectly satisfied that the Government was condemne4 from the head to the sole of the foot. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Jones referred to a character in Dickens's "Hard Times" who found a muddle every- where, as being the experience of everyone who studied the workings of the present Government. The question was not so much what the Government had done or were doing, but what the Liberals were going to do to help to replace them. Were they going to try, to remove at any rate one man who very faithfully fol- lowed the lead of Mr. Balfour—the member,for the Knutsford division? They had a gentleman ready to take the lead. Were they going to support him enthu- siastically and work with a will ? If they set to work energetically they could do .a good deal in the pro- gressive districts in which they resided to rouse enthusiasm, and there would be some chance of return- ing their friend, Mr. King, to the House of Commons. (Applause).

INTERESTING ADDRESS BY MR. KING.

Mr. King had a hearty reception on rising to slip- port the resolution. He should like,he said, to endorse everything the Chairman had so well said with regard to the late Mr. Hanbury. He thought it was well there were occasions on which they could show that their political antipathies did not affect their personal feelings and regard. If there was a member of the Government who tried to introduce the blessed word "efficiency "into his department it was the late Mr. Hanbury. He (Mr. King) was an absolute stranger to i most of them n Daresbury. It must be 30 years since he first came in touch with the place when visiting some of his relations in Warrington. On Saturday afternoons, he remembered,he spent much time in walking round Daresbury and other beautiful places in the neighbour- hood. The Knutsford Division was an awkward division from a Liberal point of view. It extended from the Cat and Fiddle on the east side almost to Runcorn on the west, and the means of cross-cAntry communic a tion were not all they might be. They in the easthad made up their minds to have an opportunity of record- ing their votes at the next election, cost them what it might. Many of them felt very much distresssd at the last election that they did not have the chance of voting against the Government. So strongly did he (Mr. King)feel the deprivation of his tights asa citizen,that he pledged himself to the committee of that division that, failing anybody else, they were at liberty to put him forward as a candidate at the next election. (Applause). They had much to contend with in that division. There • was an enormous landed interest. They could not expect the owners of these ancient halls and lordly parks to be anything but Tories. They were Tories by nature. But they were not in the majority, and the Liberals had a sufficient number of electors independent of them who ought to be able to turn the scales.

===== 0002601_19030501_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
LIBERAL MEETING AT DARESBURY. I MR. A. J. KING'S CANDIDATE.

Many years have elapsed since a Liberal meeting was held at Daresbury,which is considered one of the Tory strongholds in the Knutsford division. There was, however, a very fair attendance at the public meeting which was held in the Board Schools on Tuesday even- ing, at which Mr. Alfred J. King, C.C., the prospective Liberal candidate for the Knutsford division, delivered an address. Mr. George Rodger, provost of Selkirk, and formerly of Newton lank, occupied the chair and amongst those present were : Miss Bessie Dutton, Messrs. Josiah Longland, E. Lloyd Jones Charles Roylance, W. H. Sherburne, E. England, John Darby- shire, and Joseph Smith (Liberal agent.) i Mr. Roylance,in introducing the Chairman, said it was a pleasure to meet him on that occasion. He was sorry when they lost Mr. Rodger, because when he was , in their midst they always knew what he was doing,and where to find him. He was a true friend to the Liberal cause, and was looked up to by the whole of the neigh- bourhood as one of the leading lights. Although that, . meeting seemed a small one it was a select and an im- portant one. They were marching to the tune of pro- gress, and were determined to go ore. The cause was a , good one, and could not be beaten. It had done much for the nation' and the people generally. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Rodger then took the chair and called upon Mr. Smith to read a letter of apology for non-attendance from the Rev. J. S. Hughes. In doing so Mr. Smith said he hardly knew how to address the chairman for he believed he now occupied the honourable position of Provost of Selkirk. (Applause). Mr. Smith read the letter from Mr. Hughes and also said that had it not been for absence from home they would have had pre- sent Captain Crosfield, Mr. Z. Armitage and Mr. A. H. Crostleld. (Hear, hear).

THE CHAIRMAN'S SPEECH. THE LATE MR. HANSURY.

_ 1 The Chairman who had .a cordial receptionsaid it was with pleasure that he found himself again in their midst and surrounded by faces he knew so well. For 17 years he lived at Newton Bank and that was a good num- ber of years out of any man's lifetime. A man could not spend that number of years in a place without having c, ' a very warm interest, not alone in the locality but in every man and child he knew in it. His wife and family thought of it even more than he did, and they found much pleasure in living among them. They were met as a Liberal party, but he was sure, there was no one present who would not agree with him in saying that, notwithstand- ing the fact they were Liberals, they had a very great respect for their,competitors for the favours of the people. They would bear him out; when he said how sorry they were to learn of the death of Mr. Hanbury— (Hear, hear,)—who was 'one of the best men in. the Cabinet, and a man who showed himself determined to push farmers' advantages inevery way to the front. He , had done a great deal not perhaps in carrying through, so much as setting an example, as to what ought to be done by a Minister of Agriculture. He was sure they were all sorry to hear of his sudden death, and sympathised with his relatives in their great loss. There was no doubt that the Liberal party had been in the background , for a very long time, and that, possibly, was not good either for them- selves or the Conservative party. He rather thought they had been feeling that distinct setting back of the Liberals for the past7ls years as not redounding to the advantage of even the Conservative party. (Hear, hear.) They even found Conservative papers continu- ally grumbling, and they saw numbers of Conservative members voting against the Government of late. They had also obtained seats formerly filled by Conservatives, and votes for the Conservative candidates had been reduced; and those for the Liberals increased. Those facts point.tal to the same thing, namely, that whatever it was from, there was a feeling throughout the country that a change of Government might be of advantage to the people, and of course they, as Liberals, naturally thought so. That a change would be of advantage to the country went without saying. For a very consider- able time they had felt that the Conservative party had become "stale," and that there was a want of grip about them. Money was being wasted, and the watch- words of the Liberal party were perhaps more wanted now than ever before, namely, "Peace, retrenchment, and reform." (Applause).

MR. LONOI:i.ND AND THE EDUCATION :ACT.

Mr. Josiah Longland moved: "That this meeting congiemn.s the conduct of affairs by the present Govern- ment as contrary to the best interests of the British Empire, as reactionary and retrograde at home, and aggressive arid extravagant abroad." He was more concerned,he'said,with that part of the resolution which referred to the conduct of affairs at home. It 'did not require many words to convince them after what had been said by the friends of the present Government, as to the retrograde conduct 'of affairs at home. One of their -own supporters had discribed the Government as a Government of "mess, muddle and make-believe,"and they could not have a better instance of that than their action with regard to.the duty upon corn. (Hear, hear.) They were assured twelve months ago that the -corn duty was hot to -be a temporary measure. It was to be the co:nmencement of the widening of the basis of taxation and also that it would be paid,not by the people aehome, but by the foreigners. Aftor twelve months, however, the C utecellor of the Exe'requer had pro- posed the appeal of that duty for Cie reason that it • lent itself to misrApresentation." No greater mis- representation ceukThave been made than that the tax would be paid by the foreigner. (Hear, hear.) If it ' was paid by the foreigner, why were they giving the foreigner £2,500,000 ? They knew very well the tax had been paid bi the consumer. Then with regard to the Educatioa Bill,that waspassed without any warrant or mandate from the constituenc,ies by a Government elected on the question of the Boer war. The Government MIS returned to power for the purpose of settling up things in the Transvaal, but they had proceeded to bring in and pass by force one of the most revolutionary measures that had been passed in the last generation. One of the greatest of American presidents said that the aim of all good government ought to be government of the people, by the people, for the people. (Hear, hear.) How could they have that if the people wars not to be represented in the authorities which they constituted. They were assured also by some of the greatest supporters of the Government, and most .emi- tient members of the Cabinet, that the people would have the very .fullest control over the education of the country. The fullest control they had,seemed to be that they were entitled in an indirect fashion to select about one-third of the school managers. They had done away with School Boards as such, notwithstanding their assurance to the contrary, and now the ratepayers might elect men to Corporations and District Councils thinking that they had a knowledge of education and would make good members of the Education Committee, but they had no . guarantee that they would be on the Education Com- mittee because the authority elected the members of the Education Committee. Therefore, they had no con- trol over them or the actual expenditure of moneys. Tee education of the country should be a purely national affair. (Hear, hear.) Everybody was entitled to have the best education that tould be provided, so that all men had an equal chance. They had not yet had an opportunity of seeing how the educational bodies would work, but there was no doubt that in many instances, if the electors were asked to vote and to select men to represent them on the Education Co- mmittee they would select very different men to many who had already been elected. (Hear, hear.) The Government ought to have bought the schools or perhaps better still, ranted them. If they .had been rented the owners of the schools ought • not to have retained the control of them. He didnot agree to the introduction of dogmatic religious teaching in day schools. Sunday schools existed for that purpose, and parents and guardians of children were far more com- petent to give that religious instruction in their ovier home than could possibly be done in a -mixed school. If any religious education- was wanted to begiven, it should be given either after or before the ordinary work of the school. Perhaps in the evening would be better. The Education Bill was not a national Bill, and did not give the ratepayers the fullest control over education. Liberties which their forefathers fought for, and which they hoped to retain, had been infringed. Milton said that he valued the liberty to speak and utter freely beyond all liber- ties. But they would remember what took place dur- ing the recent war. The Prime Minister told them that if they- gave vent to opinions that were not in conson- ance with the general views of the people, they must expect to be interfered with. Was that the right to speak what they thought freely? It was desirable that they should be alive and do their best when the time canter, as it would do sooner or later, to return a representative for that division who would not only vote but speak also on the side of jus- tice. (Hear, hear). With regard to affairs abroad they only got out of the Venezuela "mess "with great difficulty, and it might have cost them the friendship of America. Referring to the sugar duties he said the cost to everybody who used sugar was Ld. per pound. The Government had since entered into a convention with Germany by which another id. in the pound would have to be put on sugar. There was scarcely te manufactured article of food but what contained sugar, and the imposition of the sugar duty had made a slight difference in the cost of these articles. With very little difficulty the Government could have made a difference in the remission of taxes by which they could have doneaway with the duty on sugar, but they had given if to the people whom they regarded as their friends in the shape of a large remission in the income tax. The Govern- ment had brought the income tax down from fifteen pence to eleven pence. They might have reduced the income tax to some extent and left enough to do away with the. duty on sugar. (Hear. .hear.) It behoved them to send representatives to Parliament. who would endeavour to carry out the old watch-words of "Peace retrenchment and reform." (Applause.)

MR. E. LLOYD JONES AND THE GOVERNMENT.

Mr. E El;;CijO-nes, in seconding the resolution, said everyone felt that the Government were on their last legs. . Mr. King: They ought to be. . Mr. E. Lloyd Jones: As had been depicted in a

===== 0002601_19030501_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DRINK INTEREST.

He understood in some parts of the division there were influences quite as powerful, more skil f ully and carefully organized for their own defence. He referred to the great drink interest—an interest whose fortunes were bound up with the fortunes of the present Government. He noticed that at a meeting of the trade at Preston, the Chairman, Mr. Groves, observed that they might trust the Government, and in fact he knew that they might trust the Government, which was largely dependent upon them,and was pledged to deal with their case at the first opportunity. There had been a great deal of excitement and panic in the country during the last few months. By a strange coincidence and while being absolutely disconnected with it, the magistrates awakened to & sense of their duty and their powers at the same time as the Government passed the Licensing Bill. The magistrates found that it wasquite possible for them to reduce the number of public-houses in distrlets where there were too many. The result of that had not been very great, only about 200 being taken away altogether,yet the "tnrie" got into a terrible state of alarm, and at the Rye election it was generally sup- posed that their influence went against the Government because they thought the Government had'something to do with it. The "trade"went to Mr. Balfour,andhe made a speech which he (the speaker) thought he ought not to have made. He went so far as not only to condemnthe bench of magistrates but attempt to bully and dictate to the Quarter Sessions as to what course they should take in revising the de- cisions of the magistrates. Then they had Mr. Butcher's Compensation Bill. He did not think any of them wanted to do anything unfair to any class of their fellow-subjects, but they were bound to look to the interests of the people as a whole. They could not get away from the fact that a terrible amount of misery and a great many unhappy homes were caused by the drink traffic. The 'drink bill wasl6OmillionssterlingorC4 per head of the population. As regarded co mpensati on,the y must lay down the rule that compensation must not be given out of the public funds, but out of the funds of • the trade." They must also lay it down that no act passed through Parliament should have the effect of conferring a legal property in a licence. (Hear, hear.) He was further of opinion ' that liceiising questions ought to be in the hands of direct representatives of the people. (Hear, hear.)

SOC lAL INFLUENCES.

In that division theyhad to fight against social influ- ences. He knew parts Of the -division where to be-a, Liberal was to be scarcely respectable, and to be a Radical was to. be outside the pale of civilization. (Laughter.) They Must be like Galileo and 'care for nothing of these things. There was nothing fighting so much on their side as the record of the past seven years of Tory rule. Turning to the

===== 0002601_19030501_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
FOREIGN AND COLON( AL POLICY

of the Government, he said that although Liberals were not agreed on the subject, the one point above all others which had brought him once more into the field of political action was the war in South Africa. He believed that war was an unnecessary one.. (Hear. hear.) What did the author now say about it? That it was the result of misunderstanding on both sides. There was not a misunderstanding whieh careful diplomacy ought not to have foreseen and avoided. (Hear. hear.) The war had been waged in the interests of capitalists in South Africa men who considered the British flag the greatest asset, but who cared very little for the honour OP that flag. He used to be able to remember the wars that this Government had waged,but they had now got so numerous that he had to write them down. They were now engaged on an expedition against the "Mad" Mullah, but personally he was not at all sure that the madness was all on that side. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) - He had not been abhi to find anyone who could tell him what business we had to be there at all. They were all grieved to hear of the disaster to some of the troops engaged in that expedition, but after all they had no business there. Of what use was that policy of imperial- ism to them? Solomon had said that the fool had his eyes on the ends of the earth, and the 'present Govern- ment seemed to have their eyes there. (Laughter.) Those who interested themselves in home affairs were termed C Little Englanders," but he thought the term was applicable the other way, because the great heart of I the nation was really at home and their island was the 'heart, and if the heart was not sound of what use was it to extend their dominions? It was said that those ' extensions of territory were necessary in order to find new outlets for markets. If they took the territory that had been acquired during the last 20 years they , found that the proportion of trade shipped to their own I colonies was rather a decrease than an increase in spite of it. Again, the Imperial policy was a frightfully ex- pensive business. When the Liberals went out of power in 1895 the combined cost of both branches (army and navy) was 46 millions per annum, but that , had now increased to 35 millions for each branch ' He thought it was Mr. John Morley who said it was a poor kind of insurance which practicallystarved the children in order to pay the premium. (Hear,hear.) Then there was the Budget. He was glad to see that the iniquitous and harassing corn tax had been done away with. It was not only a tax on the food of the people but a tax on some of the raw materials of indus- try. They had now removed it, and had also reduced the income tax. He did not want to impute bad motives, but a great Conservative paper had said that the back- bone of the Conservative party was the middle class income tax payer, and that nothing lesethan a reduction of 3d. in the pound in the income tax would stiffen the backbone. Well, they had taken off foutpence. They had still to pay duty on sugar, and they would have to i pay still more n consequence of the Sugar Bounties Convention. In order to assist a few planters of sugar in the West Indies,the Government had made an arrange- ment to increase the Value of sugar in this country a id. per pound which would cost the consumers some- thing like £8,000,000 per annum. This would not put one penny in the Exchequer but would perhaps put about £250,000 in the pockets of a few planters in the West Indies.

===== 0002601_19030501_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
EDUCATION.

Referring to the education question he said it had often been said that the only people who had done any- thing for education were the Establishedrhurch. As far as he could read history he found exactly the reverse was the case. So far back as 1807 a Bill was in the

House of Commons which, if passed, would have estab- lished national education throughout the country. It was a Bill to enable a school to be established in every parish on unsectarian lines, but it was throw* outin the House of Lords. However, the Lancasterian Society was formed, and for many years it did excellent work in founding undenominational schools. The Church party then took alarm and another society was formed with the title "The National Society for the Education of the Children of the Poor in the Principles of the Established Church." From that there was the en- deavour of the church to have control, and on the other side the endeavour to free education from the hands of the priests. The question of

===== 0002601_19030501_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
LABOUR REPRESENTATION

was exciting a good deal of comment at the present time. There were people who seemed to be afraid of that movement, and thought it might injure the pros- pects of the Liberal party, but for his part he rejoiced to see that the working classes—or the labouring classes —were taking an interest in politics at all. If they could get more men in the House of Commons like Mr. Burns, Mr. Burt, and Mr. Fenwick, it would be better for the country and everybody. If with the introduc- tion of those new members there should be any unneces- sary antagonism to the Liberal party he would be sorry, because he felt that the principles which the Liberals supported were the principles which would benefit the masses. (Hear, hear.) All kinds of freedom in many things had been secured for the people by the Liberals in the past, and therefore he thought that there should be no antagonism between them and the Labour party. He for one would rejoice to see labour more strongly represented in the House of Commons. (Hear, 'hear.) The present Government was a Govern- ment of "mess, muddle and make,—believe," and incapable of any useful service to the country in the way of administration or legislation. They had failed in even the_primary duties for which a Government was formed. It was because their policy was guided by no fixed principles and inspired by no lofty ideals. They flattered themselves that the battle of Free Trade had been fought and won, but it was not so. They also fo Ind that they had also to pay rates to support a school over which they had not complete control, and which had two doors, one to go in and the other by which to go out into the Church. The control of the Commons over the public purse was becoming a solemn farces That and much more was the price they were paying for their political apathy, their ignorance and stupidity; and their neglect of the lessons of history, which would have taught them what to expect from a long lease of power to the Tory party. Let it be a.waming to them; and remember that the price of their liberties was eternal watchfulness, as they knew not when the next election would come. The evil the Tory party had done in seven years would not be undone in seven times seven years, and should they be allowed a further lease of power he doubted if it would be in the power of any Liberal Government to restore the country to its old proud position in the world. (Applause.) The resolution was unanimously carried.

SPRING ERUPTIONS AND THEIR CURE.

Spring eruptions and spring langour are the great evils of the present season. Pimples and skin eruptions may be called "big,little things." They're little in size and big in effect. One little inflamed pimple is enough to spoil the look of some inches of fair skin. Pimples mean that the blood is loaded with impurities which require to bc expelled. The blood is the real seat of pimples, and this should be thoroughly cleansed. Charles Forde's Bile Beans purify the blood thoroughly, and not only purify the blood, but purify those sources of impurity—the diges- tive system and liver. It's no use to filter water if you run ip over mud again, and it's no use to purify the blbod if it is to be forced through a liver which will make it foul again. Miss Annie H. Gould, of 67 Spencer-road, Stoke Newington, says:—"l had nasty pimples and blotches all over my face and body, and I could not get rid of them. RESIGNATION OF ALDERMAN

SHAW GREEN.

Our readers, we feel sure, will hear with sincere regret, that Alderman Shaw Green has resigned his position as an alderman of the borough, in consequence of continued ill-health. • This step was reluctantly taken on the advice of , his medical attendant. By his resignation Alderm'an Shaw Green closes an active and honourable association with the Corporation of sixteen years' standing, and the town loses a public servant whose place it will be difficult to fill. He was first returned to the Town Council by the electors of the North-West Ward in Jubilee year (1887),and on the redistribution of the town into wards he became the representative of Bewsey Ward. One of Mr. Shaw Green4s earliest and most useful works as a member of the Connell was the part he took in the acquirement of and transfer to the Corporation of the r; Waterworks undertaking. This monopoly had long been in the hands ofa private company and it was felt that the time had arrived when the Corporation should have the control of the water supply in its own hands. Mr. Green's knowledge as an accountant enabled him to give valuable assistance in the negotiations which led to the acquirement of the undertaking and the subsequent promotion of the Corporation Water Bill in Parliament.

As Chairman of the Finance and Estate Committee, _Alderman Shaw Green was perhaps better known to the public. His yearly budgets were looked forward to with interest and he always succeeded in presentia a clear statement of the financial affairs of ttil; Borough. When he was unable, through ill- health, to present the Budget, a month ago, regret was not. only expressed inside the Coun- cil Ch i Chamber but n many quarters outside. Mr. Green also performed much useful work as Chairman of the School Attendance Committee and this was very. properly acknowledged last month when , the, committee ceased to exist and handed ' over its duties to the new Education Committee. It was a further tribute to his worth that the new authority should unanimously select him as Chairman of the Sub-Committee which will have the supervision of school attendance. Mr. Shaw Green also took an interest in the work of the Museum Committee, and was Chairman of the Library Sub-Committee, and a valuable member of the now defunct Technical Instruction SA-Committee-. He also fulfilled the duties of returning officer for St. Austin's Ward.

Alderman Shaw. Green's letter, resigning his posi- tion,.has been acknowledged with keen regret by the Mayor and the communication will be laid before the members of the Council at the first opportunity. If the Council follows the procedure of electing as alderman the Councillor who has given the longest cnitinuous service on the Corporation, it will fall to the lot of Councillor Arthur Bennett to succeed Alder- man Shaw Green as an Alderman of the Borough. This would create a vacancy in Howley Ward. • A reporter of the " Examiner" called upon Alder- man Shaw Green at his house in Winmarleigh-street on Friday afternoon, and had a brief chat with him. The alderman appeared to be slightly batter. . He talked freely of his resignation and stated that he deeply regretted that the state of his health had compelled I}im to sever his connection with the Corporation. He would have preferred, he said, to have remained in the Council .and defended himself 'against those who have sought-to cast opprobrium upon him. He was given to understand, he continued, that his impending re- moval from the town tO the country had been seized upon as a pretext for stating that he was removing in order to avoid the borough rates. No such motive, he added, was actuating him. He was removing to Latchford at the urgent request of his medical adviser and his family in the hope of recruiting his health.

MR. A. H. CROSRELD AT CANNES. GOLF WITH THE CANNES CLUB.

As our readers are probably aware Mr. A. H. Oros&ld has been for some time playing golf in Cannes, where he took a team of English golfeis to engage the local olub. There has not been much information received here as to the proceedings in this charming spot, but,pm article in a recent issue of" Golf Illustrated ." by Mr. H. H. Hilton supplies all wants. The article is chattily written and discourses at length on "A trip to Cannes." ' It is also acoompanied by an excellent portrait of Mr. Crostield. At the outset Mr. Hilton says :—" When H.I.H. the Grand Duke Michael of Russia, at the Open Championship meeting at Hoylake last year, requested Mr. Arthur H. Crostieldito take out a team of golfers to play against the Cannes Club in the follow- ing March, the request was readily acceded to. But there are many months between June, 1902, and IVlareh, 1903, and many things may happen in those months; but Mr. Crosfleld is a man who, when he has once made up his mind, seldom fails to accomplish his purpose. Early in the year he commenced getting his team together, and if he had succeeded in obtaining the ser- vices of all those originally invited, there would have been a ccmbination who would have been quite able to hold their own against any other band of amateurs in the world. The very names had quite a disturbing effect on the nerves of the secretarr of the Cannes Club, Colonel Woodward, who plaintively remarked, "Really, we don't want to play all Europe." His plaint was heard, as Robert Maxwell, John GOFF, and T. M. Hunter could not manage the journey. Charles Hutching was, unfortunately, again disabled, and at the last moment Osmund Scott was unable to come a.crpss from Biarritz. Still, with the Hoylake trio at the head, and T. R. T. Tarver as fourth man, there was the nucleus of a good team. As events turned, out, not quite good enough to carxy away the Challenge Trophy —presented by H.I.H. the president—but still suffi- ciently strong tb save their honour in a halved match. Cannes is a truly delightful place when you arrive there, but I think that all those who travelled across on Sunday will agree with me when I say that the journey is not quite so delightful. True, the sea was in a most kindly mood, but the spirit of unrest seemed to have entered the body of that wretched train as it appeared to be performing all sorts of acrobatic feats out of pure wilfulness, particularly when the wanderers were.trying to snatch a few hours' rest. But there is an end to everything and it was with a feeling of greatrelief to all when at last we reached Cannes. A short rest, and we were away to the links. First impressions are invariably a great factor in the opinions one forms of a links; and personally I must , say that the first impression was a very favourable one. llt was an extreme4ly agreeable surprise to find really good green turf where anticipations had suggested hard- baked turf of an entirely different colour. The turf certainly was hard, and troubled one or two of us con- siderably, particularly when pla.ying from the tees; but by degrees we became acclimatised to the peculiar con- ditions, and any little failings could not in any way be accredited to the links, as they were in excellent con- diti on, and it was quite possible to play very fine golf upon them, as John Ball, jun., demonstrated during the week."

The play in the matches against the Cannes Club, is described at length, the writer thus referring to the performince of Mr. Crosfleld in the singles :—" The captain of the team, Arthur Crosfield, who, owing to not being able to give the same attention to the Royal and ancient game that he usualljr does, was not particu- larly confident as to his ability to stand the assault of his opponent, Harry Bacon; but he rose to the occasion manfully, as he always does when chances appear against him, and finished with a substantial balance in his favour, and it may truthfully besaid that the greater the task the better he tackles it." After the play the visiting team were entertained to dinner at the Cernle de l'Union.

The Grand Duke, in a most happy speech, gave Mr. Crosfield's team a kind and cordial welcome. He ex- pressed his pleasure at se b eino, th them ere,and he hoped to have the pleasure of seeing them out there onanother occasion. He also, in an equally kind manner, proposed Crosfleld's health, as captain of the team.

Mr. Crosfteld, in reply, thanked his Tmperial Highness for the cordiality of his welcome, for all the kindness and forethought he had shown them. To his old friends, Mr. Ryder Richardson and Mr. Janion, he was indebted for helping him to get together a team to try conclu- sions with the Cannes Club. It had been a pleasure to assist in arranging this match. After alluding to the due course which—thanks to Colonel Woodward's inde- fatigable efforts—had been provided for the visitors, and the unique charm and attraction of golf at Napoule, Mr. Crosfield described the club as the most representa- tive in the world. At that moment it wasperhapimore representative than even Cannes had ever been before. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Wigham, they all knew, had spread the game north, south, east, and west, and not long since he had been playing over a links in Persia, where the hazards were chiefly formed out of the ruins of Nebuchadnezzar's palace. One member of his own team had even carried the game further afield, for the Hoylake, when formerly occupying an important diplomatic position as Consul for the Sandwich Islands, introduced the game there; and the Queen of Sheba— (laughter)—he begged pardon—of Honolulu was so interested that he understood she and the Consul had a game together, which she greatly enjoyed. He had before ventured to suggest, and he now repeated his suggestion, that if an artist with the necessary imagin- ation were available, this interesting event should be commemorated by a portrait on the walls of the Royal Liverpool Club of their secretary and her dusky majesty in fall native garb. (Loud laughter.) Mr. Crosfield again thanked his Highness their president on behalf of his team and himself for a delightful visit.

EGYPTIAN-SUDAN MINERALS.

Geological and other Reports on the Property owned by the Company.

The following are extracts from a report by C-aptair McCormick on the property of the Egyptian-Sudm Minerals, Limited:—

"Darenth Castle, March 23, 1903. "I enclose areport and plan on your property. I am bagging all the ore from Lake's Shaft, and, should it be required, could send a sample home... I will not write you any further account of the developments of these mines, as the report herewith gives every particular. "Road to Red Sea.—Mr. Jennings Bramley (the Government officer attached to the party) has marked down for me a road to the Red Sea which passes a per- manent spring of water. The distance would be about 00 miles. I will explore this route, and report on it at an early date. During the past three days I have 'been out with Mr. Bramley exploring the Waddies that run into the Waddy Alkakli,in the Jebel Elba concession,and found about five miles from the Alfowie Mines equally import- ant old workings in the Wady Nefrium. The ancients have worked an immense lode of quartz from the bottom to the top of a jebel about 500 ft. high for a distance of 800 yards or more. The ancients have worked an immense open cut right along the entire course of the lode, and have filled up their working with the tailings and ore they have not crushed. I got colours in pan samples, and can only conclude, froin the enormous amount of work done, that this 014 Nefrium mine is quite as im- portant as Alfowie, which it in nearly every way resembles. Any party. exploiting Alfowie could at the same time explore this mine. There is abundance of water at Kamultel within four miles. We get our drink- ing water from this place. "Mr. Kay reported at lunch that he has coming in at the bottom of his shaft another and much wider lode, which carries nice prospects. Lake's vein carries now visible gold wherever von break it from, and, in my opinion, will widen with depth, and when it junctions with one of the flat lodes which seem to run at regular intervals from the main lode, as per plan will be very rich"

To summarise a report by Mr. G. Graves Gifford: (1) It is conclusively shown that the ancients worked on very rich shuts of gold, and that they extracted all the gold they were able to do. (2) The old workings are in too dangerous a state for further working in them. (3) Examination of the lodes proves the con- tinued existence of gold in them. (4) For reasons given above, only a portion of these extensive workings have been explored and surveyed. (5) The work now in progress is designed to open up the lodes below the deepest part of the ancient workings. (6) In the new shafts leaders have been met with, especially in Lake's Shaft, of such a nature as to afford us every encourage-, ment and satisfaction, and to indicate the extreme pro- bability that, when the lodes are cut in the lower levels, the rich shuts of gold which have, been worked above will be found again at. a depth. In conclusion, may I say that we have the fullest confidence in trusting tha. the rate of progress we have made, and the pleasin results which we are able to report, will afford th directors every satisfaction. Changes on the North-Western Railway. Mr. Emmett, superintendent of the Waggon De- partment of the London, and North-Western Railway at Earlestown is retiring from the company's service in August, and is to be sneceeded by Mr. H. D. Earl, present manager of the Crewe Works. Mr, Treve_ thick, son of the first locomotive superintendent o the North-Western Railway, will be Mr. Earl's sucf- cessor as works manager, and Mr. Honifray, of Carlisle, will probably take Mr. Trevethieles place as assistant manager.

The Examiner, Friday, May 1, 1903. FASHIONS OF TO-DAY. i

Now that the modes have become more settled one cannot but be struck with the fact that save in the details of dress and a tendency towards exaggeration, the fashions of this year do not differ very greatly from those of last season. It is true that the line of the shoulder and upper arm is more closely defined, and that the fulness below the elbow is greater than before, but the skirts, however full they may be, still closely define the lines of the figute and are as graceful as ever. It is in the trimming of the gown and the deep- ness of the fanciful cuff which keeps the ful- ness from coming close over the hand that the hall mark of this season's dress is to be

WOMEN'S CHAT.

rafeir Majesties' visit to Scotland for the purpose of holding Court at ancient Holyrood, was talked about a year or more ago, and it seems strange that the Palace should have been allowed by the Board of Works to remain in an unsanitary condition. Lord Leven the Lord High Commissioner, would hardly have declined to reside at Holyrood on this account, during the sitting of the General Assembly, had it not been that the Palace is in an unfit state for habitation.

.... No one can doubt that the splendid new Railway Hotel in Princes-street affords more comfortable quar- ters and surroundings, but Scottish sentiment is justly offended that, whilst enormous sums are spent upon Windsor Castle, Buckingham Palace, and Marlborough House, Holyrood Palace should have been left in such a neglected condition as to be dangerous to the health of people staying i,there. If Lord Leven Melville is justified in refusing to sleep under its roof, the Palace can scarcely be in a proper state to receive their Majesties, even for the purpose of holding brief Court ceremonies.

Lady Francis Balfour, who protested so strongly against the Lord High Commissioner removing his Court from Bolyrood to a Railway Hotel, is a sister-in-law of the Prime Minister, and of H.R.H. the Duchess of Argyll. It is therefore pretty certain that he protest will not be in vain, and that as soon as possible the drains and sanitary arrangements at Holyrood will be put into proper condition. The Palace could not, of course, be transformed into a desirable place of residence, from a modern point of view. Such alterations would destroy its historic-1 treasures, and even if they were possible. Holyrood has been spoiled, just the same as the Tower of London, by modern surroundings. When these places were the seats of State and power, there was no such science as that of sanitation, and since that time both Holyrood and the Tower of London have become submerged in working class districts where the scenes of ancient Royal revelries are now crowded with breweries, gas works and factories. The new premises of the Royal School of Art Needle- work, are very magnificent and several members of the Royal Family were present at the opening ceremony, at which the Prince and Princess of Wales presided. The show-room, the work and class-rooms are all spacious and well lighted as are also the kitchens and dressing- rooms. Princess Christian has taken a very practical interest in the fitting up of the building, and opened the exhibition by the Company of Broiderers,which WEIS the first to be held in the new premises. It is also hoped that either the Princess of Wales or Princess Christian will be able to open the exhibition of the Home Arts and Industries Association, which will he held at the Albert, Hall on May 2.lst, The wearing of egret plumes is the subject of a fresh protest by the Society of the Protection of Birds—To seethe susceptibilities. the great majority of emporiums adopted the rule of soiling all osprey plumes as imita- tion, or artificial ones, but an investigation carried out by the Society showed that practically all the plumes on sale at the leading drapery establishments in London, were real ospreys. They were bought in the ordinary way, and with one or two exceptions, the saleswomen assured the purchaser that they were artificial,but after- wards Professor Ray Lankester' to whom they were submitted, declared them °neural all to be the feathers of the egret taken at the time when the death of the parent bird means the starving to death of the young ones. It would therefore seem that the only course open to women who desire to put down this practice, is to refuse to wear these plumes, no matter what may be said as to their origin. For wear with linen or hollandmorning gowns, there are being built canvas coats in ivory and 4cru tints trimmed with coarse crochet braiding. These little coats are quite short, reaching only to about two or three inches below the waist, shot silk being the lining usually employed. Linen promises to be one of the most fashionable of stormier fabrics, trimmed with coarse lace.

In en-tout-eas handles, the round knob and the crutch varieties are in greatest favour,and as regards materials mother of pearl, and tortoiseshell take front rank. Silver handles are no longer tolerated by the best dressed women. In the first place they are much too popular to please "exclusive folk, and then they spell ruin to all white or light coloured gloves. Anything eccentric is passed over this year, being deemed, and rightly so, out of place and vulgar. Hats for country and river wear are many and varied. The old type of sailor is still to be seen, so also is the French matelot sailor, but in leading establishments, the perfectly flat plate shape ranks first, in this particular kind of headgear. No trimming of any kind is permitted for such a hat, but it is raised more or less sharply on the left side with a bandeau formed of ribbon or flowers. The new sunbonnet, built of frilled muslin, with long strings, and a " curtain " behind, is both pretty and becoming, but it is likely to be much too popularas% the summer advances to 'be considered good style. _ .

----"American Household Management" was the subject of a lecture the other day by Miss G. Stewart, at the Women's Institute in London. No doubt the Americans are much more advanced than we are in labour saving methods and appliances, but these often mean a sacrifice of comfort or healthfulness that we should not like to put .up with. Thus their system of heating all the rooms with hot water pipes, and radiato' is from one furnace in the basement, which is attended to by an out- Ale man, night. and morning, saves an immense amount of work, but it is not nearly so healthy as the open fires and flues. They also save a good deal of work by using a common washing basin, in the bathroom,instead' of providing water and washstands bleach bedroom, but then this differs little from washing at the sink, which is not a practice that one cares to adopt. Another device that is common in American houses, is to have the kitchen and dining room adjoining, with a sliding panel or "buttery latch," through which the plates and dishes are passed. This is a convenience, but it generally means that the conversation can be heard equally as well in the kitchen as in the dining room. Most of these "improved methods " havertheir drawbaCks, and after all, the secret of successful house- hold management may be summed up in a very few words, namely" do not live in a larger house than you can easily manage." As an introduction to the NewHisotory Reader, a book of stories from English history,*ritten for quite young childt,m has been published by Messrs. Macmillan."rhe stories told range from the days of King Arthur, to the crowning of King Edward, last year. MADGE.

A SMART COATES:

noted. The trimmings consist of gauging, tassels, fringes, and lace motifs and insertions, while the short sac-like bolero, to which by constant association our eye has been trained to approve, (is distinguished by its capes and collars. I have seen and admired some very smart little coatees of this order made of biscuit or putty-coloured chine silk accordion pleated with frills of chiffon to match inter- mingled, and a collar of lace of the same tone. These coatees were generally finished in front by long ends either of a stole-like description or made of the accordion pleated silk finished by lace or a silk fringe. A bolero of this style is very useful for afternoon wear, whether walking or driving, for it can be worn over any sort of bodice and with almost any description of skirt. A smart specimen Is gilitn in my sket:ll, Of courge to some People such a lone of colour would not prove becoming, and I should in such a case recom- mend that black silk should be substituted, or, if preferred, silk of the same shade as the skirt which accompanies it. Still tha biscuis colour _is really smarter and looks cool and nice in summer -worn with a voile skirt of the same tone and a tucked tulle oitoque. _

Blue is a very fashionable- colour this season, and there are some very smart toilettes 'pi this shade, a touch of black being generally introduced into the scheme of colour. Indeed Madame La Mode who has smiled so approvingly upon that graceful flower, the fuschia, likewise approves of the

curious, but lovely, red and blue colour of this flower for a complete toilette whether used in a harmonious combination or taken singly. In the realms of millinery there are some smart hats for both morning and after- noon wear. There is the absolutely flat round, hat, which takes the place of the sailor hat, and looks very modish when worn with the hair dressed. high. To be correct it should be tilted slightly over the face. ,It is best suited to a round plump face and a piquante style, and should not be worn by anyone with a long thin face. Another simple and smart little straw hat is the canotier with the brim at either side turned up sharply or when preferred the brim is turned .up at one side only. A rosette or bow of ribbon and occasionally a feather forms the trimming. _

A pretty picttire hat is depicted with a graceful and undulating brim which would suit any face. One seldom sees a cache- peigne on the best models, although occasion- ally a small bow of la e'e or ribbon is allowed to rest on the hair at the back. The doom of the cache-peigne was of course sealed when it became so generally worn as when fashioned

_ -'RETTY BLOUSE: by unskilful fingers or worn by an unsuitable pepson it lost its charm and was therefore discarded by all the best dressed women. . Blouses are still worn by everyone, and one cannot but marvel at the charming models shown. The latest mode is that they must be en rapport with the costume and that they must likewise e harmonise with the circumstances in which 'they are worn. For morning wear there are some very charming fine white embroideries used for fascinating yet simple shirt blouses, while black and white batiste trimmed with coarse ecru lace is considered very chic. Some of the blouses which are popular incline towards the bolero shape and are trimmed with lace or fringe and have a wide cape-like collar and a deep folded silk waist-band. The great feature of some of the newest blouses is the great fulness of the sleeves below the elbow where they are decorated with gaugings or cordings. A lovely blouse is depicted and in this inAance several shaped frills take the place of the fulness below the elbow, the under sleeve consisting of a tight gauntlet of lace which covers the wrist. The front of the bodice is box-pleated alternated with bands of lace insertion, while a silk cravat is interlaced down the centre. The effect is very stylish. It is easy on regarding these elaborate blouses to understand the reason of .the great popu- larity of the cape-like coatees which are now and will , continue to be worn on into the summer. A lighter style would not allow for the disposal of the full sleeves and there- fore those who prefer the plain sleeve peculiar to the real tailor made coat will have to content themselves with a simpler style of blouse which has a less exaggerated style of ‘leaveJ

Local Examinations. At a monthly meeting of the Council of the Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes on Tuesday a report was submitted relative to the number of exercises worked at the recent examinations, showing a total of 46,103, being 82.7'per cent. of the number for which application was made. The Duke of West- minster has become a vice-president. Workhouse Inmates Entertained.

The inmates of the Workhlarse spent an enibyable evening on Saturday when they *were entertained by St. Hilda's Minstrel Troupe. The fist part of the programme consisted of sentimental and comic songs. The sentimental-singers were Messrs. A. Grounds, T. Brierley, J. Shakehaft, T.' Grounds, W. Wright and Master W. Rhodes, the latter being loudly encored for his song, " I dreamt, my little boy, of thee."- The comic singers were Messrs. J. Isherwood, J. Ediersley, J. Grounds and H. Skelliorn, whose songs and funny joke's created great amusement. The second part was opened with a laughable absurdity entitled " Brannagan's Band," after which"; character songs were rendered by Mr. J. Isherwood; who con- tributed " The wrong man " and Mr. Mohan " The Ballymoney Conversazione," and both were heartily encored. The entertainment was brought to a close with a sketch entitled "A Manager in a fix." The parts were taken by Messrs. E. Norbury (manager), H. Tongs (Sambo the • servant), J. Grounds (Sammy Slapcabbage), A. Grounds ('Enry Skriven), H. Skel- hoim. (cornet soloist)' E. Leigh (the Japanese warbler), J. Isherwood (Madame Tolostie), and E. Birchall. Mr. H. Millwood discharged the duties of pianist and Mr. Warburton those of interlocutor.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must mall cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday.

Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one. We cannot under ordinary circumstances undertake to return the manuscripts which are not used.

' Penketh Parish Council.

Sir—May I ask your indulgence to air a little (Pries.- ance, which I think affects the village at large ?• see in a recent issue that Mr. John Midwood has been elected on the above Council to fill up theposition made vacant by the death of Mr. R. Garnett. • I am quite aware that the existing Council has power to fill up a vacancy, but I think it is their duty to select a gentleman who can undertake the duties in a satisfactory manner. I would ask is Mr. Midwood the best man in thevillage for the posi- tion? I say that there are as least a dozen gentlemen, business men, and who through long residence in the parish know its needs, who could have been selected. Were they asked to stand? It is a well known fact, and much commented upon, that the Council, with on! exception, is composed of members of the Wesleyan Church. In view of the new Education Act, and its workings, as well as other general parish matters, it would be much better to see the representation divided a little. It should therefore be the duty of the ratepayers in Penketh to take a more intelligent inter- est in parish affairs, and see that a representative and capable body of Councillars are returned at the election next April,and matters will then be more satisfactory to all the parish, including, yours, etc., " DROTT,"

Compensation for Unrenewed Licences. • Sir,—ln dealing with this question it is not well to allow the social evils, (such as excessive drinking?, to influence our judgment as to the legal or moral claim of those who have not their licence renewed solely on the ground that the licensed:."ouse was not required. . .

- Any claim upon the nation that has either legal or I moral sanction ought to be respected by the nation. The sale of intoxicating liquors is recognised,as well as restricted by law, and those engaged in it can justly claim to be protected in all their rights. What then is the legal and moral right to claim com- pensation from the nation for unrenewed licences? First as to the legal claim. Prior to the definition of the law in the case of Sharpe v. Wakefield there appears to have been some doubt as to the legal position, but this was put beyond doubt by the judgment of the highest court. It was laid down. that the tenure of a licence was an annual one, that the magistrates had full power to refuse a renewal or rather to decline to grant another year's licence whenever it seems to them inexpedient for the public good, and when such a licensed house was not required by the reasonable necessities of the district, in which such house was situated. Since the judgment was given twelve years ago, magistrates have continuously exer- cised their powers. Last year over 200 applications were refused to those who had formerly held licences, solely on the ground that the licensed hottseswere'not required. So much for the legal aspect of the balm. Let us now examine themoral aspect. It is said, insupport of this,that custom gives no moral claim, that licences have been bought, sold, valued, assessed in the supposi- tion that they would be renewed. I have already indi- cated, that for at least twelve years, it has been the custom of the magistrates, to exercise their legal right to suppress unnecessary licences,theargument therefore on the ground of custom breaks down, when the facts are considered, and if licences have been bought and sold, without a discount being taken off for uncertainty of tenure, the buyers have only themselves to blame, because they cannot claim ignorance of the conditions under which all licences are held. It ought to be re- membered that this uncertainty of tenure is one of the ihevitable conditions associated with a restricted trade. The same reasons which make the general restriction Idesirable, call for alteration from time to time. Examples of the alterations in the character of this restriction may be found in the limitation of the hours lof sale, the partial closing of public houses on Sunday, I the closing of back doors, Ste., but no compensation has I been given or claimed on account of these.

We need not here enter into the necessity of this re- striction,but would point out that it gives to those engaged in the trade a monopoly and the profits of a. monopoly, and it is unreasonable for the trade to ex- pect to enjoy all the advantages of a restricted monopoly, and to escape the drawbacks incident to it. Alderman Francomb, in a recent utterance indicated he was of opinion that in certain areas, it would be for the good of the trade that some of the public houses should be closed, as he believed that the same amount of trade would be done by those that were left. No doubt the Alderman was partially right, the houses left would largely reap the benefit from the licences pure- newed, but it is going too far to say the same amount of trade would b done. Drinking and drunkenness is to some extent the product of its opportunities, and the right that the community has always exercised,in the restriction of these opportunities must be preserved and it must never be asked to pay for a right which has always been its own. But apart from all this,it may be asked: Is it not a hard position for a licensee who has had his licence renewed year after year and no fault is found against him in regard to the conduct of his house,to have the renewal de- clined, solely on the ground of there being more licensed houses than the needs of the locality require? Why should one be taken and another left? Undoubtedly there is a hardship, a hardship akin to that of the man, who through no fault of his own has his premises and stock turned to smoke by the ravages of fire. I fully admit the misfortune to the individual,but the question we are discussing, is not in regard to the loss, but who should make the loss goo d?Certai nly not the taxpayers but the trade which enjoys the benefit of the monopoly. It is for the trade and for the trade only, to consider, how an insurance fund to meet the individual hardships is to be raised. Mr. Butcher in his Bill now before Par- liament. sums to some extent to recognise this, but instead of leaving the licensees to insure the possible loss, he would have the State recognise the claim and meet it by a tax on licensees based on the percentage of the value of their licensees, further proposes to augment this fund by the appropriation of any further tax which may be put on intoxicating liquors. It is difficult to under- stand how 266 members could be induced to %lite for a bill so radically bad. It is a bad bill first, because it would in future make any reduction of licences depend, not upon the needs or wishes of the inhabitants,but upon the amount available in the compensation fund, wkich in many cases might be utterly insufficient; this would paralyse the action of the magistrates acting for the community. Secondly it is had, because it takes away the right of the community to tax, for general purposes, intoxicating liquors, and mortgages, imperial taxation for the benefit of a class who have no moral or legal claim upon the state. Thirdly, it is bad, because it establishes a vested interest in licences, which has never before been recognised,and this endows one of the richest monopolies in the land. CHARLES E. PARKER. Penketh, April 29th, 1003.
