Concerts In Alley and Court.

Sir,—Being extremely anxious to see developed in your town (with its many, destitute and unfortunate) whathasnowbecome so riMrked a success in Liverpool and many other towns, namely, the court and alley open- air concert movement, conducted from mid-May to mid-September, I write with a view to placing before your readers some of the very desirable advantages aris- ing to the poor from this endeavour. Finding that in no other way could the refining influence of music be brought to bear upon the very poor, the Food and Betterment Association originated the idea in Liverpool six years ago, all anticipated good results being more than realised. Apart from the grand decorative recep- tions invariably extended to these " court musicians " at least in Liverpool—continued increased cleanli- ness has been noticed, a lessening of desire for the coarser kind of pleasures with a corres- ponding growth of delight in the one so pro- vided.and a visible encouraging tendencytoemergefrom low conditions into higher. Strong evidence support- ing these statements, I trust your readers will kindly give the matter the attention it truly deserves, letting me know if any of them wish.to begin this uplifting work, when I shall be glad to furnish all necessary information for start ing . —Yo urs, &c., H. LEE J. JONES, (Founder and Hon. Director, Food and Betterment Association(. Limekiln-lane, Liverpool, April 24th, 1903.

The Workshops Competition has again been carried on very successfully, and was won on Wednesday even- ing, by Messrs. Oros&ld's team, who beat Rylands' men by 10 points to 3 points after a good game. Excellent attendances have prevailed, and at the final there must have been about 4,000 people present, the gate-money realising £2B in sums of 4d. and 2d. It is expected that when the accounts are made up a handsome sum will again be available for the charities of the town.

WARRINGTON RUGBY FOOTBALL CLUB.

===== 0002601_19030501_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
Review of Season 1902-3. By " Ovalist."

The cycle of events has once more brought round the end of the football season, and it devolves upon me to again review the doings of the Warrington Football Club during the crowded eight mouths from September to thq.end of April. It has been an event- ful period, but less so for the 'Warrington Club than for some others, though the locals have had their sensations. Generally speaking, the Warrington men can look back upon the season of 1902-3 with a fair amount of satisfaction, notwithstanding that there has been nothing gained and a cup has been lost. These latter facts would seem to be a:reason for des- pondency,but the prevalent feeling is that Warrington have not done badly. I can not,of course,congra,tulate them on any great achievement, but they have held their own in the Severe struggles of the Northern League, and the club that regularly holds its own in these days of keen competition is not to be sneered at or commiserated with. Cups and things cannot be looked upon as trophies to be had for the asking, nor can it be expected that any one team will be at the head of affairs for more than a single season. With the exception of losing the South West Lancashire Cup to Runcorn, who have fairly won it, Warrington have shown no falling away, but have, instead, slightly improved their position in League football. Other teams of greater 'tote cannot produce so consistent a record. For instance, Broughton Rangers, who last season secured every possible honour and won three cups,have fallen from their high estate so far as to be only fifth in the League table, and entirely minus of all honours. Yet they have had the same men and the same resources as formerly. " 'Tis not in mortals to command success,"but they can "deserve it,"akid the only club which has deserved success and obtained it, is Halifax, the proud winners of the Northern Union Cup and the Northern League Championship. War- rington have once more finished up near the centre of the table—as a matter of fact they have gone one better and are slightly nearer the top of the League than the bottom, while the number of points obtained is just one in excess of the number of matches played. A point per match is Warrington's usual average, and they have this time slightly improved upon it. The scoring power aid defence of the club both, however, show some deterioration, for out of 26 League matches last season, 162 points I were obtained against 150 points, while 34 matches this season have only produced 148 points against 161 points scored by "the other side." The cause of this falling away I shall discuss later on. The season commenced with a severe defeat of 12-0 by Swinton, which was followed by a scratchy win over Brighouse Rangers. Then the team found its feet, for the visit to St. Helens resulted in a victory by 18 points to 5 points, the most decisive of the season as it eventually proved. Runcorn at home was the fourth engage- ment and a pointless draw was the result. The return engagement saw Warrington receive their severest drubbing, 18-0 being the sum total of the disaster. After the first month, Warrington proceeded in moder- ate style, until the large proportion of away matches began to tell its tale. A draw at Halifax was the best thing done during this period. By the end ofs the year Warrington's fortunes appeared at a very low ebb, and the club figured so very near the bottom of the league that there were ominous whisperings about the second division, while the committee caught it hot from almost all quarters. The club had, how- ever, been more than ordinarily, unlucky, for in addi- tion to the many away matches, it was rarely that a full team could be put in the field owing to various causes. Fish and Isherwood were absent for some time through injury, while the latter's fellow- centre, Dickenson, underwent six weeks sus- pension for a breach of the working-clause. There was not much improvement noticeable until the middle of February when tha team was again at full strength, and there was a plethora of home engagements. Since the 24th of that month only two defeats have been chronicled, against seven victories and three drawn games. The Club climbed steadily in the League until the present creditable position was reached. On only three occasions has the club suffered defeat at home, the conquerors being Salford, Bradford and Wigan. The first named club is the only one to take four points out of Warrington this season, and to- gether with Warrington, shares the honour of taking three points out of the champions—Halifax. Warring- ton's defeat of the latter club on Good Friday was the achievement of the season, performed too, before a record crowd. This completes .the tale of Warrington's accomplishments, and now for the failures! Fortunately they are few. First and foremost they have lost the South West Lanca- shire Cup. Then they have also suffered defeat in the first round for the Northern Union Cup at the hands of humble St. Helens, and finally they have failed to strengthen themselves as a team. The same men are doing duty who have upheld the Club for some time and though they are young in years they have had too much work put on their shoulders. New blood would have been very useful, but the only " capture " is young Mahoney of Cardiff, a young threequarter who has never played with the first team and whose abili- ties remain to be proved The team has played good football—excellent at times—but has all along been, handicapped 1)y one thing--the weakness of the forwards. It was just the same a year ago, and' there is no question whatever if some good big men had been secured in place of the moderates who do duty in the . Warring- ton pack, the team would have made a .name for itself. The backs are good enough for anything, and it is only the fact that they have been on the defensive so much that has kept the scoring average so low. It. is a matter for reproach to the Committee—who may have the best excuses—that the wordiness so long apparent has not been in the slightest degree reme- died. The remarks I made in this column a year ago are significant and merit repetition. Speaking of the forwards I said "about 50 per cent. of new blood is urgently required. The scrummagersfhave quite failed to uphold the club's reputation either for strength or cleverness, and a big improvement here is a task which the Committee must at once set about perform- ing." Is the need any the less now? I say emphati- cally : No,it is greater ! Owing to the full programme of League engage- ments this season the Warrington club has not played a single" friendly" match. The League table therefore contains a summary of the results of every game Played. There have been 31 matches altogether; 14 have been won, 13 lost, 7 drawn, with a total of 35 points. The scoring has been 23 goals, 34 tries for, and 28 goals 36 tries, against. Warrington have beaten twice, St. Helens, Batley, and Widnes; once, Brighouse Rangers, Halifax, Leigh, Hull, Oldham, Kingstou Rovers,Hunslet, and Huddersfield; drawn with Broughton Rangers (twice), Swinton, Halifax, Bradford, Runcorn, and Wigan. Salford, as above stated, are the only team who had beaten Warrington twice. Swinton,llradford, Runcorn, and Wigan took three points out of Warring- ton. Last season, Warrington only drew one game— with Widnes at Warrington.

WORKS COMPETITION FINAL.

Soap v. Wire.

Striking evidence was afforded on Wedilesdav even- ing of the increasing popularity of the Works' com- petition when the final tie was played off. 'The attendance was equal to that of a first team league match and Mr. Treasurer Pemberton will have a nice round sum to -hand over to local charities. The two teams to enter • the final were Crosfield's and Rylands, so that the Titanic struggle may be said to have been between soap and wire. There were plenty of parti- sans on both sides and .they were easily distinguish- able, especially among the younger element. Those who pinned their faith in the superior qualities of soap sported a card in their caps, enjoining their re- presentatives to "play up," whilst on the other hand those who favoured wire displayed an appropriate design with the word Rylands woven in wire. Punctually at 6.30, the referee, Mr. J. IL Smith, of Widnes, gave the signal for the start. The teams lined out as follows : Crosfields —W. Lawton, jun., Bradshaw, Kinder and Dutton; threequarter backs; Howard and H. Lawton, half backs ; Wray, Dickinson, Caldwell, Lear, Roy- lance and Warburton, forwards. Rylands—Smith, fullback; Sutton, Penketh, Wright ' and Brown, threequarter backs; Floyd and Morris, halfbacks; Philli Higham, Henson, Simister, Mason and Broadhurst, forwards.

The game had not been in progress many minutes before Crosfields scored a good goal, the result of a clever drop kick by Roylance. A loud cheer rent the air and mingled with the cheer was the discordant note •of some hundreds of tor baloons which the youngsters' blew with vigour. -Shortly afterwards H. Lawton sc-n-ed a clever try under the posts and the lead . of the soap men was further increased when W. Lawton sen., kicked a goal. Soap was now in the ascendant in popular favour, and the men, encouraged by these successes played with greater dash. As the. result of a beautifully combined m we- ment, Bradshaw scored a try for Crosfield's., The. supporters of wire-Were now getting a little despond- ent, but they endeavoured to urge on their men to renewed efforts. It was evident, however,that wire had been badly twisted, but there were still hopes that the wire-men would give a better account of themselves. In the second half they had several opportunities to score but all with one exception were not taken advantage of. The one exception .was really a clever piece of combined play and the try registered .by Penketh, one of the Rylands' team centres, must .be considered the best of the day. When • the whistle sounded for time,Crosfield's retired winners :of the gold medals and the losers received the silver medals as a solatium,on the evening's play. Crosfield's were the better team and were much quicker. Several good openings were lost by the wire men through lack of judgment and want of combination. DISTRIBUTION OF THE MEDALS. The vote was carried with acclamation. ; I ' PRINCESS MARY'S Iltcyct.E.—Princess Mary of Wales was six years old on Saturday, and among the presents was a tiny Rudge-Whitworth bicycle, given by the King to his grand-daughter. About three years ago, his, ; Majesty presented Pritice "Eddy " with a similar machine, and since then, the princess has essayed riding her brother's bicycle. Princess Mary is ,perhaps the youngest royal cyclist in Europe. The dimensions of the bicycle are lilliputian. Only IGin. of tube separate the bracket and seat pillar. The rims of the wheels, which are 201 n. in diameter, are made of aluminium. The diameter of the tyre is an inch and a quarter, and the machine is geared to forty-four. On the front of the plain black enamelled frame appears a small" M" in gold. 'The whole,weighs only 151bs. Prince" Eddy" and his sister have not yet been allowed the delights of • a free-wheel.

The Penketh authority had decided to leave the matter in the hands of a sub-committee, and should the Sankey Council adopt a similar course he would be pleased to arrange for his sub-committee to meet the Sankey representatives. It was decided that the Clerk reply to Mr. Foster to the effect that in the opinion of this Council the ques- tion was one which should be referred to the landowner.

PERSONALITIES AT GREAT SANKEY.I Lively Parish Council Discussion. MR. HALTON ALLEGES "INSULTS." A lively scene occurred at the annual meeting or the I Great Sankey Parish Council, which was held in the Board Schools on Tuesday evening. At the outset Mr. George Thompson occupied the chair, and. there were also present :—Messrs. J. Fulton Smith, H. Singleton, Thomas Halton, J. H. Kilshaw, William Taylor, and Joseph Smith (clerk). THE ELECTION OF CHAIRMA.N. MR. TAYLOR ELECTED BY A. CASTING VOTE.

The Chairman said he had great pleasure in proposing that Mr. Taylor be elected Chairman for the ensuing year. Mr. Taylor was an old member of the Council and the appointment was only what he deserved. Mr. Taylor said he would rather propose that Mr. Thompson be re-elected. The Chairman declined to stand again. His health had not been what it should be for some time past and he had really thought of resigning altogether from the Council. He hardly liked to do so as that was their last year in office, but it was very likely that he would not be able to retain a seat on the Council after this year. Since last November his health had been anything but satisfactory, and during the last month he had been very ill. He hoped Mr. Taylor would accept the chairman- ship if elected. Mr. Singleton said he was surprised at the Chairman's decision. He had intended to propose Mr: Thompson's re-election, but under the circumstances would second the election of Mr. Taylor. Mr. Kilshaw moved an amendment that Mr. Fulton Smith be elected. Mr. Smith had fulfilled his duties as a member of the Council most successfully and he (the speaker) was sure be would be capable of carrying out the duties of Chairman. Mr. Halton said he felt himself placed in a difficult position. He regretted to hear the remarks the Chair- man had just made with regard to the state of his health. He was not aware Mr. Thompson was so unwell, but he was sure it was the hearty wish of every member of the Council that he would soon be restored to health, and continue to take an interest in the business of the parish •as he had done in the past. He seconded the amend- , ment, as Mr. Fulton Smith had done a great deal to further the interests of the people of the district. He ' was sure Mr. Smith would occupy the position with dignity and attend to all the duties of the office.. Before the motions were put to the meeting, Mr. Halton said he thought Mr. Thompson was a little out of order in occupying the chair. The Chairman: I think lam quite in order. The chairman is chairman until his successor is appointed. Mr. Halton: I think not. The Clerk upheld the Chairman's opinion-1 and in support of his ruling, quoted from the Act of Parlia- ment dealing with the matter. Mr. Halton said it was the rule with other bodies that he had been connected with for the chairman not to occupy the chair whilst the voting for his successor proceeded. The Clerk : I think there- is either an Act of Parlia- ment or seine regulation of the Local Government Board which provides that for certain authorities the Clerk shall take the chair when the new chairman is being appointed. I don't think that Act or :regulation applies to Parish Councils. The Chairman: If I had been a candidate I should have Vacated the chair, but in view of the Act—and I have read it through many times—l shall remain in this chair until my successor is appointed, whatever may be said one wayor the other. Mr. Halton: .1 don't want to interrupt you and don't want to be unkind in regard to your position especially as you are unwell, but now that you say you will occupy the position of chairman I coincide in your ruling. I don't wish to take from you your position. I have only mentioned the matter because it has been custOmary for years with every undertaking that I have been coaneoted with, for the Clerk to preside over the meeting for the time being. On a vote, being taken three members voted for Mr. Taylor and three for Mr. Fulton Smith, and the Chair- man gave his casting vote in favour of Mr. Taylor. . MR. HALTON'S STRONG REMARKS.

Mr. Halton: I think the Chairman has stolen a march on certain members of the Council. It looks on the face of it as if there was a certain amount of cliquism connected with the undertaking.• Mr. Thompson: I object to such lan_guage being used in this room. There is no cliquism. I have no men- tioned the matter to any person. Mr. Halton: Allow me. lamon my feet. I won't allow von to interrupt me. I sin here as a representa- tive of Great Sankey, and I won't be interrupted by von. You must understand you are only one individual. 'You do not occupy the chair, and von must not interrupt me. It is for the Chairman to call attention to matters, and just keep to the point, will you please. The Chairman: Be quiet, please. Mr. Halton: I say that Mr. Thompson has shown himself very disagreeable to the members of the Coun- cil this evening. I have comehere and I may say I have received insults from Mr. Thompson from time to time ; in fact as far as regards my position,if I had not been elected by the representatives of Great Sankey to further their interests I should have retired long since, because I don't think when you are elected by a body in the interests of the ratepayers you should be inter- rupted byau individual who has a .pertain amount of personal spleen against you. I say it fearlessly to any- one living, and I think I have colleagues who can bear .me out by saying that it is correct. I did not come here to waste my valuable time and to be insulted by any individual member of the Council. It is a great insult so far as my position is concerned to come here time after time to be insulted by Mr. Thompson. It is a well known fact, and has been for a long time, and I think he is wrong in doing it. I have worked on his behalf, and assisted in placing him in the position of chairman, and I think he ought to be the last in the world to try to ignore my position in Sankey. I don't know what it is for. I think it is a " cast " upon him- self, but not upon me, and it is cawing it a bit too far. Mr. Thompson: • I don't know what this is all about. came here to-night to carry out the business of the meeting as far as the chairmanship was concerned,. and I have done so. I had no arrangement, and there was no cliquism. No one knew what I was going to do. I hardly knew myself, but I had a wish that Mr. Taylor should be chairman as he is an old member of the Board. lam sure I have not spoken to Mr. Singleton or to Mr. Taylor or any other member of the Council with reference to this matter. I cannot understand Mr. Halton, and as to insulting him I never insulted him in my life and never wish to do so. I wish to live on friendly terms with everyone. Mr. Halton: Question, question. It does not look like it.

WARRINGTON PHOTOGRAPHIC SOCIETY.

Exhibition of Members' Work.

A Criticism.

During the week a photographic exhibition has been held at the Old Academy under the auspices of the Warrington Photographic Society, the pictures being entirely the work of members. A careful inspection of the ninety-odd examples of what can be done in the art of the camera by our local amateurs leads us to the con- viction that .the Society possesses some studious and original photographers among its members. The exhi- bition,as a whole,k very creditable,and we hope to see it continued each year. It hasistruck us,however,that,seeing the exhibition has been thrown open to the public, a committee of selection might have been appointed to supervise the exhibits before they were hung. A certain amount of judicious weeding,out might have been done with distinct advan- tage both to the exhibition and the Society, for there is no question that many prints now on the walls should never have left the seclusion of the dark room. They are not calculated to stead either the cold light of day or the keen criticism of the onlooker who knows any- thing of photography. It is possible to overlook some of these anviteurish efforts—Which still do credit to the industry of their producers—but they unmistakably detract from the excellence of the exhibition as a whole. There is tune for the consideration of this and other poiuts,ere the Society again invites the public to an exhibition of members' work. With regard to the majority of the pictures there are many worthy of note anda good number of exhibitors are to be congratulated on the proficiency they have attained. We do not favour comparison, but it is impossible to altogether avoid i t,in the course of a criticism such as we feel bound to make. The conclusion we first came to was,that unquestionably the best work in the room was that of Mr. H. Bond, whose landscape and figure studies are remarkable, equally, for technique and artistic conception. This is an opinion we would have preferred not to express,seeing that Mr. Bond is coil- nected with the "Examiner," but as our criticism is . meant to be above all else, candid and fair,we felt bound to give Mr. Bond the credit we think is due to him. His landscape "Morn on the hill-side," and portrait of a girl reading, appear to us distinctly best in their respec- tive classes. Mr. Podmore, however, receives first award in the landscape section for "Ploughing" (velox) the caviller of two prints of the same subject. He has undoubtedly made a happy selection of subject here,and reproduced it with a naturalness and pictorial quality deserving of high praise. But why two prints? It is a test of the exhibitor's artistic perception for him to decide on the tone, paper, and size ofs his picture, and a lack of decision in this respect detracts from the value of his work. There is another picture by Mr. Podmore, "Evening Mists," which we deem as bad as the other is good. It is too fuzzy even for evening mist, and is so obviously touched up in the centre with chinese white or some other pigment as to be irretrievably spoiled. His "Lonely Church" is altogether too lonely, the foreground consisting solely of a haystack and a five-barred gate which appear to have been dropped " promiscus "iii the field. These ,three pictures, too, have all been taken under the same sky—a rather re- markable effort on the part of Nature or Mr. Podmore! One other of Mr. Podmore's examples is a charming view of the Horseshoe Falls, Llangollen—quite the best , reproduction of this subject we have yet seen. The Rev. E. E. Ferrer has a very pleasing picture—with a blank sky unfortunately—entitled "Fairy frost work," , andldisplayine some of the most delicate tracery of ' fern, shrub and tree by the master-hand of King Frost. His "Winter, blank and cold " is not so good, however, the broad expanse of snow and the straight fence render- ing the scene commonplace. Mr. Kertland has several fair landscape enlargements on view, the best being the "Canal, Llangollen," and the worst "In a pasture green," where we see three cows staring blankly out of the picture, and looking as if they had been, like the trees, planted. There are two attempts at wave photography, by Mr. K. F. Bishop and Mr. H. Miller, both of which are highly creditable. The one by the first-named, "There's a storm coming up," we like the better, the water conveying clearly the idea of motion. Among the curiosities, and, there are a few, is a "Sunset" by F. W. Purse—a small print looking feral! the world as if the youngest member of the household had done something disastrous with the ink-pot. Two "Views of Blackpool," by J. V. Moore, are very good, the snap-shot in sunlight being excellent, but for the one fact that the shadows are too deep. The other is ratherflat. There is, of course, no attemPt at the pictorial here but as photographs of a prosaic subject they are distinetly praiseworthy. Two views of Grappen- hall,by Mr.J.Newton and Mr.P.Kellyeasoadorn the walls. Mr. Newton appears to us to have obtained the more pleasing result,)iis picture being greatly helped by the two bonny youngsters stepping along in the foreground. Among the portraits are some excellent exhibits, one of , Mr. Bond's we have already alluded to,blit further corn- plhuentary reference is merited by "The Gossips," a Dovedale composition excellent in all respects. Mr. W. J. Twiss has been .venturing on the difficult Rembrandt style and his portrait of "An old man" is very fine, the tOp-lighting being well-managed, and the whole effect very delicate and lifelike. Another, of "An old woman," with one side of the face in shadow is much inferior. We need the catalogue to distinguish the sex of the sitter, and the print l is also harsh, with the contrast between the two sides of the face border- ing on the extrprne. Mr. G. Nichols has a full length portrait of Mr. W. L. Benner, to which we must award praise. The likeness is excellent, the pose good, and the modelling of the face very successfully delineated. There are three portraits by Mr. W. Daintith, but they are faulty in many ways, and all are worsened by th inartistic mount which this exhibi- tor has chosen for his prints. One portrait is of an elderly gentleman against a background of plants, the leaves of which form a murderous looking chereaux de frise round his head. 'rhis is not the royal road to artistia portraiture. In the architectural section there is likeivise a good show, but we have no love for this class of photography. Messrs. Bishop, Kertland, and Armstrong have, however, obtained some excellent results. One picture by Mr. D. S. Birrell seems out of place in this section for there is little of the architec- tural about "A remnant of the past," which is a pleasing view of quay-side shipping, printed in carbon, the tone being suitably subdued. The title also seems to fit badly, but this is perhaps the fault of the catalogue? . Messrs. T. and Birtles have kindly undertaken the . task of judging and awarding the honours to various exhibits. Their awards are as follows: LANDSCAPES : Ist. Mr. W. H. Podmore, scene , "Ploughing"; 2nd, Rev. E. E. .Ferrer, " Fairy Frost Work." honourable mention Messrs. Bond, Kett- land, and W. H. Podmore. . . ARCHITECTURAL let, NTr. J. H. Kertland, "Valle Cruets Abley"; 2nd, Mr. K. F. Bishop, "Queen 'a Gate, Mt. Orgniel Castle"; hon. mention, Messrs. J. J. Armstrong and J. H. Kertland. PORTRAITS AND FIGURE STUDIES: Ist, Mr. H. L Bond, study of a girl reading; 2nd, Mr. W. J. Twiss " An old man."

The exhibition was opened to the public on Tuesday afternoon, in the presence of a large number of ladies and gentlemen interested in photography. Mr. W. J. TwtsB, one of tho hon. socrotaries,remiuded Mr. FALRHURST that he was the first president of the old society, .which was established in the year 188 G, and that he was also the first president of the re-organized society. He asked him to open the exhibition. Mr. FaintlunsT said he had only had a casual look round the room—he would take advantage of other opportunities of he was pleased to find that a remarkable improvement had taken place in the work of the members since the time he used to photo- graph. There were many specimens on the walls he had never approached in point of merit; in fact, he did not feel capable of turning out such good work. He was pleased to be present to see the revival of the society, which must be an interesting fact to the members-gener- ally. He hoped to see the exhibitions continued .year after year, and the society become more and moressuc- cessful. He had much pleasure in declaring the exntbi- tion opea. (Applause.) - Mr. E. J. Hm.r„ one of the vice-presidents, in respond- ing to a Call, said he was delighted to see such a heautfiil collection of work on the walls, and he most heartily congratulated the exhibitors on the results which had been obtained. He might also congratulate

the society upon having such a plicasant room in which to arrange the work ; such a room lent beauty to the I.exhibition. He hoped with Mr. Fairhurst that the society would be exceedingly successful, and that large strides would be made year after year until it had be- come a recognized institution of the town. Mr. TWESS, in proposing a vote of thanks to the judges (Messrs. T. and J. liirtles), said they had crm- tributed to the' success of the exhibition by their awards: Mr. -FAIRHURST seconded, and said that while the duty of judge was not always a pleasant one, he felt -certain that both Mr. Males and his son had done their ' duty conscientiously and well,3vith regard to the pee- sent exhibition. (Applause.) , The motion was heartily agreed to, and a vote •of thanks having been given to Mr. Fairnurst for his ser- vices, the formal proceedings were brought to a con- clusion.

The exhibition will be open to-day from 2 unti 10 p.m.

Coming of Age Celebrations.

lOn Monday iTight the coming of age of Miss Annie ' Hill, A.V.C.M"., of Napier-street was celebrated in Brick-street Mission Schoolroom. About 60 friends and pupils responded to the invitation. After the reception of the guests,tea was partaken of at 6 p.m., the tables being' well filled and laid out in tasteful style. After, tea,parlour games were heartily indulged in and thornighly enjoyed by all prssent until 10.30 after which supper was laid. Miss Hill was the I recipient of a large number of ornamental and useful articles which were displayed in the schoolroom and greatly admired by her Many friends. A most successful and enjoyable evening was brought to a close about 12p.m.

THE LATE MR. E. W. T. RICHMOND. The Treen Cycling Fatality. Deceased's Insurance.—Arbitration Proceedings.

(SPECIAL TO THE " EXAMINER:I

An Arbitration Court was opened at Penzance last week, the case .to be settle l being a. claim brought by the executors of the late Nfr. Z. W. T. Richmond against the .Commercial Union Assurance Compapir • Ltd., for z£2.,000, the amount for which deceased had, insured his life, alleged to have become payable ea his death through ,a cycling accident at Treen last. summer.

Mr. Colan, Rec)rder of Croydon, instructed by Mr. H. Hatton, of Messrs. Robert Davies and 0., of War— rington and Mr. Edward Boase, of Penzance, appeared for the executors, and Mr. Rriegg, instructed by Mr. Payne, of Manchester, and Mr. Cr. L. Bodily, of Penzance, appeared for the Company.. The arbitrator is Mr. H. E. Dake, K.C., Recorder of Devonport. • Mr. Ruegg made an application that the case should be heard in private, Mr. Colan saying he did not object to newspaper reporters being present, but he, did not desire their presence either. - Mr. Duke said that unless both parties agreed to the presence of re- porters, it was not custoina7 for arbitration cases to be reported, and accordingly the members of the press present then retired.

- There was a large number of witnesses called by both sides, including the famous cyclist, Mr. G. P. Mills, Dr. Atkins, the deceased's brother-in-law, Mr. R. P. Couch, ex-Mayor of Penzance, the local C.T.C. Consul.

In support of the claim it was urged that there Was no justification to suppose that Mr. Richmond was guilty of negligence to such an extent as to invalidate the insurance policy. Evidence was called to prove that after the accident the front wheel was buckled back almost to the fork, but the back wheel was perfect and the back brake worked efficiently. It was impossible to say whether the back brake was temporarily jammed by a stone, or whether the bicycle swerved inlpassing vehicles or struck a stone. The machine after the accident, showed a scratch on the nut of the front axle, corresponding with a mark on the wall of a culvertlwhere Mr. Richmond was flung showing that the machine must have collided with it. A visit was paid by the Arbitrator to the scene of the accident. He was accompanied by Counsel and Mr. G. P. Mills.

The defence set up by the Insurance Company was that the machine was practically brakeless. It Was alleged that the composition blocks of the back brake were worn very irregularly, the inner edge of one block being worn down to the holder whilst on the other side there were no signs of wear, as if it had never touched the rim. It was claimed that Mr. Richmond was aware that both brakes were useless and that he made an ad- mission at the Lands End Hotel, before starting that he would have to walk all the hills.

Addresses on the evidence will be delivered by counsel in London, probably at the latter part of nest week, or in the following week. The arbitrator will probably give his decision within a fortnight.

1.0.0. T. Temperance Mission. Lodge. The weekly session of the above lodge was held in' Ebenezer Church, Buttermarket-street on Wednesday. Bro. P. Bate C,T., presided. Bro. Marsh, of Malta, was admitted on clearance card from the "Coronation Star "lodge. Bro. Merlin made sympathetic refer- ence to the death of Sister A. Heywood and moved that a vote of condolence be sent to the relatives. Sister Mrs. Sheath seconded and the motion was carried unanimously. It was also decided to go into mourning for a period of three months. Bro. 3-- Sheath drew attention to the fact that the certificates won by some of the'juvenile templars in the National Juvenile Competitive Examination were at Liverpool waiting for public presentation, and it was decided to ask Sister Duncan to present them at a meeting to bo held on May 13th when parents and friends are cordially inv:ted. A • strong discussioo followed on the subject of "Absent meg' bers" and a special visiting committee formed to take the matter in hand. Bro. C. J. Shoat gave a report of the meeting of the No. 1 Conventions- South-West Lancashire, held recently at TyldesloP Bro. Marsh tendered the fraternal greetings of thr, "Joseph Livesey " lodge. Preston; "Coronation Star ledge, Malta; and also of Bro. Captain Webb, D.0.Z.," Military District. The lodge has now a memberTe of 66, and a special effort 7is to lie made duriAS next quarter to increase the membership, an", - possible, to double the numbers. ,

Dear Sir,—Over two years ago I b.g•in to be troubled with severe pains in my head and body,lost my appetite and could not sleep at night. I grew so irritable that I could not bear to speak or to be spoken to. I consulted several doctors in Edinburgh and Glasgow, also went to the Infirmary. They all agreed that my trouble was dyspepsia, and nervous debility, none did me any good. I tried many Patent Medicines and gave each a fair trial. I began to despair when I was recommended to try VITADATIO. I would not for some time, but feeling very ill I reluctantly pt-- chased a bottle, and before that was finished I was relieved. By the time I had taken three large bottles of VITADA.TIO my health and strength were fully restored. I now enjoy soy meals, sleep well, have no pain, and am free from the old irritable feeling. My wife and family tried VITA.DATIO for indiges- tion with good results. I recommend VITADATIO wherever I can.
